The body-part rules are ordered and the first to fire decides, so a rule ahead of another has to be the narrower of the two: `brain_neck` asks for a neck word and a brain word where `neck` asks for the neck word alone, and that holds only while the two read the same neck words. `cou`, the French for it, is three letters, and a keyword is matched as a plain substring anywhere in the text, so it fired inside ordinary words. On an archive it was never once the word a verdict cited: it was found behind the spine rule on 6 stacks and, after the wave gave the body-part rules a text with nothing dropped from it, behind the brain-and-neck rule on 35 more. Those 35 are series whose file states their body part as a brain, and a fragment of a longer word made each of them a neck beside a brain, while the rule that can name a neck sits behind that one and was reached by nothing: the axis had four values and could produce three. The word is spelled as a word now. The anatomy normalizer expands the token `cou` to `neck`, which is what the other normalizer's expansions are for, and `cou` is a keyword in no rule, so a fragment decides neither rule and the French word decides both the way the English one does. The 35 come out as the brain their file says they are, a stack that really does say both still comes out as both, and a neck named without a spine or a brain beside it reaches the rule that names it. The pack is version 0.1.5.

- A zero echo time decides nothing by the vote either, which is the route it was still deciding by. The guards the base windows were given reach a rule and not a pass, and a re-run over the same archive found the same phase-contrast study's 297 one-image fragments still called T1w on 288 of them. The path is `physics_vote`: closing the window left the base empty, an empty base is what makes a stack a target of the vote, and the vote reads the echo time again through its key, where 0.0 is binned to the nearest 5 ms and lands in the bin the short echo times of gradient-echo anatomy sit in. The flow study's own whole series, which do have an echo time and which a window therefore judged, were the neighbours it was answered from, so the wrong answer came back with a vote's confidence instead of a window's and nothing said a zero had decided it. A key dimension can now declare that a zero on it is not a measurement, `zero: absent`, and a zero there bins as a hole, which matches only a hole: a stack with no echo time votes among stacks with no echo time and usually among none at all. The MRI pack declares it of the echo time alone, because a repetition time, an inversion time and a flip angle of zero are as impossible but nothing measured says what declaring them would move, and a key is shared by every stack in an archive. The fragments now come out as what the pack can honestly say about them, the flow technique and the misc directory type with no base at all, and they stay the one question the split already raises about them. The pack is version 0.1.4.

- A zero echo time is not evidence of a short one, and a split that leaves one image in every stack says so. On a run over an archive, one phase-contrast study came out as 297 stacks of a single image each, every one of them an anatomical T1w acquisition: the series split on the echo number, which varies per image on a flow study, and the base contrast was then decided by the window that reads a very short echo time, on a series whose echo time is 0.0 because there is nothing to measure. The verdict was written at 0.70, which is the confidence the pack asks below, so nothing was flagged, and that one study became 279 of the archive's 339 stacks holding the `t1w` role. Two things are fixed. Every window in the MRI pack whose evidence is that a time is *small* now asks first that the time was measured at all: the gradient-echo and spin-echo windows, the FLAIR and dual-echo ones and the EPIMix route, where an absent value already failed the comparison and a zero one did not. And a split that leaves a stack holding one image, on a series split into four or more such stacks, is a question of its own, `split:one_image_per_stack`, carrying the reason the series split, collapsed into one item per reason rather than one per stack; a series whose split gives each stack its images raises nothing, and a stack the pack has ruled out is still nobody's question. The pack carries the three cases that say what a zero decides, which is nothing, and is version 0.1.3: one version for both of the changes this wave makes to its knowledge, the second text before it and the guards here.

- A release writes no session the session layer did not derive, and an ask and a selection count one archive the same way. A series carries the subject its own files named and a study carries the subject the study's files named, and the two part company wherever an archive re-linked a series under somebody else's study: the subject that is left owns series and no study, is on no timeline, gets no session and is an open question, `identity.no_study`. The release read the session label off the study alone, so it borrowed that other subject's sessions and wrote the disowned subject into `ses-` directories no scheme produced. On a run over an archive the scheme derived 44 sessions over 39 subjects and the tree came out with 45 session directories, two of which belonged to a subject the engine itself called incomplete, so a released dataset carried structure nobody derived, for somebody the engine had already said it could not place. A label is now read only from a session of the stack's own subject, and a subject that owns series and no study is refused rather than named, on the session layer's own test: its stacks are recorded in `release_absent` as `no_session` with the reason, counted among the stacks the release left out, and the subject is named in the report, so a person who selects that subject sees it counted by the selection, no directory of it in the tree, and a line saying how many of its stacks were refused and that `identity.no_study` says why. A study of a subject that does have studies but that carries no date is unchanged: it is on no session either, and `unknown` still says what it is, because it borrows nobody. The same disagreement made an ask and a selection over one archive give different numbers of subjects, 38 against 39, because the ask reached the subject and the session through the study while the selection reached them through the series. Both now read the series, which is the row that carries the subject a stack's own files named, so a stack is never counted under another subject nor rolled up under another subject's session, and the count a selection prints is the count an ask holds.

### Added

- An ask can group by an axis. "How many stacks per technique" had no document: the grammar has always allowed `["axis", {}, name]` among a group's keys, validate accepted it, and the compiler refused it, because an axis was only ever read as a predicate through `=` or `has`. A group over a stack set now takes any axis the pack declares as a key, `by: [["axis", {}, "technique"]]`, with the axis name as the key's column, on the command line, the serve door and MCP alike, since all three run one compiler. A multi-valued axis (modifier, construct, quality, role) keeps a row per value, so how a stack with two values counts is said rather than left to the join. By default the key is the stack's values as one sorted list, the same text the `list` aggregate writes: every stack counts once, a stack saying FLAIR and FatSat is its own key beside the stacks saying FLAIR alone, and the counts sum to the stacks. `["axis", {each: true}, "modifier"]` counts a stack once under each of its values instead, which is the number `has` gives per value and sums to more than the stacks. A stack with no value falls under the null key, a stack the pack ruled out is in no group, and describe says which of the two readings a document asked for. A group's rows are now numbered with a null key last on both backends, where SQLite put it first and Postgres last, so a group whose key can be empty keeps one content hash across the two. The gate gains two fixtures, stacks per technique and stacks per modifier under `each`, and the engine's tests hold the stack with two values and the stack the pack ruled out, which the generator writes neither of.

- A stack says what it covers. The fingerprint has always carried what one slice looks like, the rows, the columns, the two pixel spacings, the thickness and the spacing between slices, and never how many slices there were nor how far they reached. Measured over 437,069 classified stacks of a legacy archive, the number of slices alone is what separates 3,610 names carrying 9,905 stacks, more than every timing parameter together: two prescriptions of one protocol over a different extent, wearing one name and handed a run index that says they are the same acquisition twice. Four columns answer it. `n_slices` counts the distinct positions the stack's images sit on, from `SliceLocation`, so a dynamic series of twenty repeats over thirty slices covers thirty and not six hundred; `slice_span_mm` is the distance from the first to the last; `coverage_source` says which evidence answered, `slice_location` or `none`, so a stack whose files never placed their slices reads as unmeasured rather than as a stack of no slices; and `acquisition_matrix` is the last of the four geometry fields the survey names that the fingerprint did not already hold. A fact meant to tell two stacks apart has to be stable, or every pair looks different and the failure is only the mirror of the one it was meant to fix, so two tolerances hold it still: inside a stack, positions within a hundredth of a millimetre of one another are one slice, clustered from the lowest up rather than rounded to a grid so that two values a hair apart cannot fall either side of a boundary; and between two stacks the comparison asks for equal counts and extents within the wider of a millimetre and a hundredth of the larger, and answers that nothing differs where either side measured nothing. The slice count and the extent are fields a comparability level may compare exactly, round to a step of its own or ignore, which is where a tolerance between two stacks belongs; no level in the MRI pack names them yet and no verdict moves in this release. A pack and the ask can both read all four.

- The fingerprint says which derivation wrote a row. A row was fresh while its stack had gained no instances, so a build that learned a new fact left every row an older build wrote exactly as it was, with the new columns empty and nothing saying so, until somebody thought to pass `--force` over the whole registry. Each row now carries `fingerprint_revision`, the derivation that wrote it, and a row from an earlier one is stale however many instances its stack still has. An existing registry needs nothing said to it: opening it migrates the columns in, and the next `nils fingerprint` or `nils intake` derives every stack again and fills them.

- A stack says which coil received it. A series is split into one stack per receive coil, and has been since the digest was written: the engine reads `ReceiveCoilName`, decides on it that these are two acquisitions and not one, and then keeps the value nowhere a classifier, an ask or a name can reach, so it manufactured a difference it could not state. Measured over a legacy archive, the coil is the only thing separating 1,082 colliding names carrying 2,412 stacks, and it differs somewhere inside 5,504 of them. The fingerprint now carries `receive_coil_name`, from the stack's own row and never the series', because a series that split over two coils holds one of them and attributing that one to both stacks would be worse than holding nothing. The value is the element as the file writes it, from `ReceiveCoilName` and, in an enhanced object, from the MR Receive Coil Sequence of the functional groups: a vendor's own name for a coil, a head array from one scanner and a short code from another, and no vocabulary is imposed on it here. The element is optional and about half of the stacks of a real archive carry nothing, so the column is null there, which is the honest answer and the same one the split already gave: those stacks were never split on a coil either. The derivation's revision moves with the column, so a registry that already has fingerprints fills it on the next `nils fingerprint` and nobody has to know to pass `--force`.

- The identities an image type states are axes, and the flags the pack parsed and read nowhere now decide something. Measured over the 437,069 classified stacks of a legacy archive, 17,065 of them, 3.3 per cent, carry a token in `ImageType` that changes what the image is and that no axis of the pack read, and in 2,829 colliding names the tokens that differ are ones nothing ever looked at. The largest was already parsed: `COMPOSED` and its five short spellings tell 2,525 colliding names apart, more than any other single fact in that archive, and they went into a flag called `is_composite` that no rule mentioned. A composed image is now the construct `Composed`, so an image stitched from several stations and the station images it was built from no longer agree on every axis there is. `MEAN` is the same story on 1,154 stacks in a dead `is_mean`: it is the image the scanner writes beside the echoes it combined, always a series of its own, agreeing with its per-echo siblings on everything the pack holds and often carrying no echo number at all. It is now the construct `EchoCombined`, and it reaches that through the susceptibility route as well, which is where 1,084 of those stacks are. `MAVRIC` and `MAVRIC_COMPOSITE`, in a dead `is_mavric`, are the technique `MAVRIC`, and the second is also a composed image of one, which is what that spelling says. `TTEST`, which separates 209 colliding names, is the construct `TTestMap`: a statistic over a series rather than an image of a person. The quantitative maps were worse off than unread. `QMAP T1`, `QMAP T2` and `QMAP PD` have been parsed apart since the pack was written and the construct axis read none of the three, so every stack whose image type said `QMAP` came out as a T1 map and a T2 map at once, whichever it was. The maps are now one exclusion group in which a map that names its quantity beats one that says only that it is a map, `FLIP ANGLE MAP` reaches `B1map`, which is what BIDS calls it too, and `has_pd_map`, `has_b0_map`, `has_b1_map` and `has_qsm` reach the values they were named after. The projection spellings that had no predicate at all join the ones that had: `MINIMUM`, `MAXIMUM`, `HD MIP`, `MAX_IP`, `MIPT`, `COLLAPSE`, `PJN` and `CPR`, 549 stacks between them, and the substring test that looked for `PROJECTION IMAGE` now looks for the part that survives the truncation the archive writes on 326 more. Four flags went the other way. `is_dixon_water`, `is_dixon_fat`, `is_dixon_in_phase` and `is_dixon_out_phase` said the Dixon part a second time, where `Water`, `Fat`, `InPhase` and `OutPhase` on the construct axis have carried it on all 2,773 stacks that state it since that axis was written; a second spelling of a decided fact is how two parts of one program come to disagree about it, so the four are gone and the cases that pin the part now assert the axis. Where any of this belongs in a filename is the next slice's question: this one is about what the row says. The pack is version 0.2.0, because a vocabulary is a contract and a question asked of one version must not be answered in another's words.

- Each axis sits where it belongs in a BIDS name, and nothing the standard refuses is dropped. Measured over 836 named stacks of the record 34 corpus and against a legacy archive of 437,069 (`studies/2026-09-19-bids-naming/`), five facts were in `acq-` that the standard has an entity for and four were being thrown away because the suffix in hand would not take theirs. The scanner's pipeline and the reconstruction kinds, MPR, MIP, MinIP, denoised, filtered and ORIG, are `rec-` now, which every suffix group the pack writes admits and which separates not one pair less than `acq-` did; the contrast is `ce-`, the complex part `part-`, the echo `echo-`, the phase-encoding direction `dir-`, and the inversion comes with the suffix as it did. What the schema refuses a suffix is no longer dropped, which used to make two different acquisitions share one name and call the second a repeat of the first: the fact is spelled into `acq-`, which is free-form, as the entity's own word and its value, so a post-contrast diffusion image reads `acq-...CeContrast` and a magnitude ADC map reads `acq-PartMag`. Where the label carries the fact already the label is left alone, which is what happens to magnetisation transfer: BIDS gives `mt-` only to `MTR`, `MTS` and `MPM`, and the `MT` modifier is an `acq-` token of this pack, so the name says it once. Either way the release counts the refusal by entity, in the report, so a tree says how often the standard had no slot for something the archive states: over that corpus 15 contrasts, 4 echoes, 3 parts and 3 transfers, closing 9 of the pairs that were sharing a name. The body part stays in `acq-`, because it is what separates 22 otherwise identical pairs and a filename has to be unique, and it now also goes where the specification keeps it, the sidecar's `BodyPart`, written into the converter's JSON after the conversion and before the tree's own digest, so a re-run sees no change. And the axes `acq-` may read are the pack's to declare rather than a list inside the engine: the quality axis this wave added could not reach a filename at all, because naming knew six axis names and that was not one of them. A pack that declares an axis and gives its values tokens has named with it, and a `from` that is neither an axis nor one of the two fields the stack measures itself, the orientation and the acquisition type, is refused when the pack loads rather than contributing nothing in silence.

- A release says what a name carries, in two modes, and records which it wrote. **BIDS** is the default: what the standard's entities carry, in the entities, and the rest in `acq-`, which is what a validator reads and a tool joins on. **Informative** carries every axis the pack declares, including the ones an entity already says, for a tree read by people rather than tools: in the BIDS layout that is a longer `acq-` label, still a legal BIDS label because `acq-` is free-form, and in the descriptive layout it is section 9.1's grammar, which has no entities to carry anything. A person chooses with `nils release --naming bids|informative`, at the command line or through the release door; unasked it follows the layout, `--naming bids` on a descriptive tree is refused because that tree has no entities, and the mode is written on the release row beside the layout, so a re-run of a release writes the names that release wrote. The pack says which mode each group of `acq-` tokens is in, `modes: [informative]` on the ones the standard has an entity for, so the BIDS name says a fact once and the informative name says it at all. And the informative echo suffix is per stack: it used to be all or nothing over a bucket, so one stack without a measured echo number dropped the suffix for everybody and the whole bucket fell to a `_1`, `_2` counter, which over 836 stacks is 78 buckets and 453 stacks, 6,997 of whose pairs differ in nothing but their echo. Each stack is named from its own echo now and what still shares a name is counted after that, which leaves 448 such pairs instead of 7,451.

- A stack says what its own file says is wrong with it, on an axis of its own. Two more tokens answer neither of the questions the other axes ask. `NAVAIL`, on 105 stacks, is a derived image the scanner produced without one of the inputs it is computed from, and it separates 93 colliding names; `DISTORTED`, on 19 stacks, is a vendor's word for an image its distortion correction did not reach, written beside the `DIS2D` and `DIS3D` siblings that it did, and it separates 15. Both were parsed into flags nothing read. Neither says what the image is, so neither belongs on an axis that answers that: they are `quality`, a multi-valued axis with the values `InputUnavailable`, `Distorted` and `Encrypted`, decided after the axes that say what a stack is and blank on the ordinary stack, which is most of them. The third value is `ENCRYPTED`, 92 stacks whose pixels nothing downstream can read. It is said here rather than by ruling the stack out, because what arrived is still a fact about the examination and what it costs is the conversion; the disposition now refuses that conversion and names the reason, and the stack keeps the disposition it would otherwise have had. Nothing else about what is ruled out changes. `is_qa`, the flag that would stop 21 standard deviation and coverage maps being classified as images of a person, is still deliberately read by nothing, and says so in the pack: wiring it is a change to what is excluded, and that belongs with the rest of the exclusion tail rather than here.

- The quarantine says what it set aside. About one file in a hundred of a study directory is an object NILS does not take: a walk of 120 directories and 140,326 files found 1,677 of them, most of a whole study's radiologist annotations in one case, and the report a person read said `unsupported_sop_class` with a UID beside it. A UID is not an answer to what the archive held, and the question a reader actually has, whether anything was lost and of what sort, could only be answered by looking the UIDs up one at a time. Every quarantined file now carries a kind in words, derived from what the refusal already recorded: the family of its SOP class where the class is the reason, secondary capture, presentation state, structured report, key object selection, registration, fiducials, segmentation, raw data, encapsulated document, waveform, parametric map, another image class, another standard class or a private class, and what the class itself means otherwise, not DICOM, unreadable, a broken header, no identifier, no modality or another modality. The digest's report gains a `set aside` block, a line per kind with its count and the classes inside it named as the standard names them, in the printed report and in the batch's stored counts, so a run says forever what it left behind. The review item of each quarantine class carries the same kinds beside its count, so the queue reads as what was set aside rather than as a number. And `nils quarantine list` prints the kind beside the class for each path, with a summary line above them and the counts in `--json`. Nothing new is read from a file and no column is added: the kind is a reading of the class and the UID a refusal already wrote down, so a registry filled before this answers the question as well as one filled after.

- An enhanced multi-frame file is read whole, and a file whose frames hold more than one stack becomes more than one stack. An enhanced object carries its per-frame facts in a sequence with one item per frame, and the reader took the first item and treated the file as one instance in one stack, as v0 did. The archive holds 1,567 enhanced series over 1.13 million frames, and a reading of 60 of those series frame by frame found about one object in ten holding more than one stack inside it: five varying the stack the frames declare, five varying the orientation, four the diffusion weighting, five an echo time or an echo number. For those series NILS ingested a fraction of what the file held and said nothing about the rest, and nothing downstream could recover it, because the stack of an instance was computed from the file alone. Every frame is now read and grouped by what a stack is made of, and a frame contributes exactly what an instance contributes: the same fourteen values, with the six that resolve through the functional groups read for that frame, the shared groups still the fallback of a frame that says nothing, and the orientation class derived from the frame's own direction cosines. Frames that agree on those values are one group, groups whose keys come out equal are one stack, and a file left with more than one stack becomes that many, each with the values its own frames stated. The instance stays one row, filed under the stack of its first frame, and a new table says which frames of it are in which stack, as ranges counting from one, so a reader of the second stack can find the frames it is made of. A file may not state more than 64 stacks: past that it is read as one, as before, and says so. The digest's report counts the frames it read and the files that split, a `frames_multi_stack` diagnostic carries how many stacks a file made, and the written block counts the rows. Grouping costs about 0.7 microseconds a frame against 1.2 to 1.8 to parse the header the reader already parses, so the whole archive's frames are under a second of processor time; an example in the reader measures it. A registry digested before this is short of those stacks and no migration can fill them in, because the frames were never read: the table arrives empty and the tree has to be digested again.

- A conflict reaches the stack it belongs to. The classifier has always noticed when one rule decided an axis and another would have decided it otherwise, and it counted those per batch with ten sample strings: a run over an archive raised 719 of them and not one reached a stack, so a person reading a stack could not see that its evidence disagreed, and a queue could not be sorted by it. Each disagreement is now a review item on the stack, `<axis>:conflict`, carrying the answer the stack holds and the answer it was decided over, each with the rule set, the rule and the word or flag it cited. One item per stack and pair of answers, and the grouping names both answers, so the same disagreement over a whole archive is one question about the pack rather than a row per stack; a stack the pack rules out is still asked nothing, as every other question is. The per-batch tally stays, because a pack's own hygiene is read in counts. The evaluator also records one case it used to keep quiet about: an ordered rule set stops at its first firing rule, so where a later rule of that set would have stored something else, and both rules read different words of the stack's own text, that is written down too, which is the brain beside a spine that a review found and the engine could not show. A later rule that fires on the same evidence as the winner is not a conflict: an ordered set puts the specific rule before the general one on purpose. Nor is a later rule that read another text: a pack may normalize one description more than once, and a word one reading keeps where another drops it is two readings of the archive rather than the archive disagreeing with itself, so a conflict is only ever recorded against the text both rules read.

- A session is never made from a study with nothing in it, and identity says what no timeline could take. A study row that holds no series is what the digest leaves where the series that declared it were filed under another study, and a rebuild turned each one into a session carrying no series, no stacks and the same labels as a real subject's; the two ways a rebuild reads a timeline even disagreed about it, since the one that reads the source's own label from a path has always needed a series to read it from. A rebuild now takes only the studies that hold something, from either reader. The rows themselves stay, because a study is a fact in a file, and each one is now an open question, `identity.empty_study`, naming the study, its subject and the day it carries. The other shape is the subject that owns series and no study, which gets no session, no `sub-` path and appears in no ask: it is now `identity.no_study`, with the series and the instances it owns counted. Neither is repaired, because both are what the files said and a run that rewrote them would be choosing which patient identifier of a study is the right one; both are said once, brought up to date by every later rebuild, and closed by the rebuild that finds the row whole again. `nils session rebuild` prints both counts and carries them in `--json`.

### Changed

- A release keeps the date (record 38). The release is pseudonymous, not anonymous, and the date is the key the clinical layer joins on, so the `shift` and `year` date policies are gone with everything that served them: the per-subject offset and the linkage store table that held it, the refusal that tied a moved date to remapped UIDs, the session numbering a dataset's declared shift forced, the report's `session_naming` sentence, and the gate's shifted trees. Where a date must not show in a path, a months scheme labels the sessions `M00`, `M06` and so on, as it already could. `nils release --dates` and the `dates` of `POST /api/releases` accept `keep` alone, for a caller from before, and refuse `shift` and `year` in words saying what to do instead, as a place's `handling.on_release.dates` does. Migration 50 takes `dates` out of every declared handling, drops the release plan's offset column and the linkage store's offsets; a release row from before keeps what it recorded and is read as it stands. A tree released with its dates kept before this re-runs unchanged.

- The ask answers the selection it prints. A stack the pack ruled out is not a stack any set sees: that is a standing predicate of the language, the description of every document says so in as many words, and the query the compiler wrote left it out. Every other standing predicate was there, a subject merged into another, an event a later one superseded, a withdrawn pick, a cohort read over the open interval, so the gap was this one and it was silent: the ask counted the whole registry while a release and the desk's own selection counted what the pack kept, and the two numbers differed by exactly the ruled-out stacks. The predicate is now part of the stack relation, so it reaches the count, the rows, the funnel a diagnosis prints and the SQL an explain hands a reader, and the number a run prints is the number of rows it returns. Any answer that read stacks on a registry holding ruled-out stacks was too large: a count of stacks, a record of them, a measure over them, and any subject or session set that kept a row because it had a stack of some description. An answer that never reached a stack, and a registry where the pack ruled nothing out, are unchanged, as are the recorded answers of the gate.

- A review threshold and the sentence that describes it say the same thing, and a run says how many answers stand on a threshold. Every threshold the engine states in words is read as strictly below, so an axis written at exactly the confidence its threshold names is an answer and not a question. That was already what the comparisons did and it was written down nowhere, while both of the MRI pack's own numbers sit at a confidence a rule always writes, 0.65 for a body part from a keyword and 0.70 for a base contrast from the timing parameters, so each takes its whole population out of the queue by that boundary and nothing said so. The comparison is now one function with a rounding tolerance, so an answer that reaches a threshold by a different arithmetic sits on it rather than under it, and the classifier, a pass and a rehearsal all ask that one function; a pick's borders keep their own words, where a runner-up `within` a fraction takes the fraction itself and a share `below` a floor does not. `nils classify` counts the answers that sit exactly on a threshold, per axis and per pass, and prints them beside the size of the queue, in the report and in `--json`, so the number of questions and the boundary that produced it are read together. The pack says in its own words that this is what its numbers do. Each threshold has a test at the value itself.

## [1.0.0-alpha.36] - 2026-09-19

### Fixed

- A container install's registry is opened by the account that made it, so a rootless podman install gets past its places. The registry was made by the engine's own image, with the mount handed to the container by podman's `:U`, which chowns what it mounts into the subordinate range the account is lent: the key and `nils.toml` came back owned by an id no account on the machine has, and the next step, which declares the places by opening the registry out here, was refused by the registry the step before had just made, with `nils.toml: Permission denied`. The install stopped there, before a quadlet was written or the pod came up, and a second run stopped at the same place, since the registry it found it still could not open. The steps that make the registry and the steps that read it now agree about who owns it: every container of the install runs as the account that made it, and every mount of the install's own directories is mounted as it stands. Docker remaps nobody and is told that account's own numbers, as it was; podman maps the account to the container's root, so the engine, the desk, Kvasir, the assistant and the step that makes the registry are told to be that root, in the commands and in the quadlets alike, and in the step that registers the desk at a provider, whose client secret the account reads afterwards. What the containers write is then the account's, and what the account writes is theirs to read: the registry, the archives, the desk's folder and the source directories an install reads, which the image's own user could not read either where the account keeps them to itself. A machine install is unchanged, and so is the Postgres a setup runs in a container, whose data directory is its own image's to own. An install made before this version, whose registry a handed-over mount left to a remapped id, is named rather than repaired: the sentence says which id the registry belongs to, which this account is, and that `podman unshare chown -R 0:0 <dir>/registry` gives the files back.

- An install signs off with the services it wrote. The card at the end of `nils setup` read its `runs` line from the runtime alone, so an install asked for the services of this machine with `--system` was planned as `systemd system units` and signed off as `systemd user units`, and one on a machine that can keep nothing running was planned with no services at all and still signed off `back after a restart`. The plan's `services` row and the sign-off now read the same words from the same place, so the two cannot disagree: the services of this machine, this account's own, the runtime's quadlets or compose file, or none, where the sign-off says the parts were started by this setup or are started by hand instead.

- `nils update` says when GitHub refused to tell it the newest version, and asks the release listing before the file. Every release so far is a pre-release and GitHub's own `latest` skips those, so `latest/download/VERSION` answered 404 every time and the version was only ever in the listing; where the API refused the request as well, an update reported `no release to update to`, which reads as though there were no release rather than no answer. The listing is asked first, which is one request instead of two, and its answer is kept whole, so a refusal can be told from a file that is not there: the update says the listing was refused, what GitHub said, that the limit is the one for an address without a token, and when the requests come back, as the hour's end where the headers give it and as the wait a secondary limit asks for otherwise. The request carries no credential, since the engine reads none for GitHub anywhere. A channel of a deployment's own has no listing and its plain `VERSION` file is read exactly as before, and so is a GitHub repository whose listing names no release.

- `nils update --all` writes the helper and its sudoers rule again from the record, as `nils setup` and a repair do. The helper answers a fixed list of words, and a version that gives it a new one, as `reapply all` was, left an install that only ever updates with the helper of the version it was installed with: reapplying every part from the desk's Parts page restarted nothing and said to run setup again as root, which is the hand step the installer exists to remove. Both are now written once the parts are up to date and before the services are restarted, so the supervisor comes back to the helper of the version it is, and only where the record names a helper: an install whose services are an account's own has none, and an update is not where one appears. A run that is not root writes neither, says so on a line of its own and carries on, since the parts are replaced by then; on an install whose services are the machine's own the update is root's anyway, because the supervisor asks the helper for `update`, which runs `nils update --all` as root. Where `visudo` will not read the new rule, the rule the machine has stays in place and the update says so, so the supervisor keeps the words that rule names rather than none at all. Setup, a repair and an update write the helper and the rule through one function, so the three cannot drift.

- A purge removes what the install made outside its own directory. `nils uninstall --purge` removed the install's directory and signed off with `NILS and everything it made are gone from this machine`, while the registry's schemas stayed in the Postgres a site runs and the lingering setup had turned on stayed on for the account. The next install on that machine stopped at the registry with `schema nils already holds a registry`. The setup record now says which of these this install made, and a purge removes those and nothing else. Where setup makes a registry on Postgres, the record names the schema it made it in, so a purge drops that schema and the `<schema>_linkage` beside it, and never a schema the record does not name: a registry this install found already there names none, a record written before this was recorded names none, and a record that names a schema the registry does not answer at drops none. A Postgres this setup runs itself is the install's own and goes with the base directory, so nothing of its is dropped or named. The drop goes over the registry's own connection, through the engine's code and not a `psql` the machine may not have, and as the account that made the schemas: for an install whose services are the machine's own, as the engine's account under `runuser`, since a Postgres that authenticates peers knows that account and not root. Where setup turns lingering on for an account that did not linger, the record names the account, and a purge turns it off again; an account that lingered before setup ran is left as it was, and an install whose services are the machine's own turns none on and turns none off. A database that refuses the drop, or a login manager that refuses to turn lingering off, is said with what it answered and the command that would do it, the rest of the purge goes on, and the last sentence names what is left rather than claiming the machine is clean. The command carries the connection string with its password masked, since a terminal, a scrollback and a log of the run all outlive the file that held it, and where a schema was not dropped the registry's `nils.toml` is kept beside the directory that goes, as `<dir>.registry.toml` and readable by nobody else, with the sentence saying where the string the command needs now is. Where every drop succeeded that file goes with the rest. A container install's registry was made inside the container and kept the address a container names this machine by, which out here is no address at all: the drop and the command a purge prints both name the database at this machine's own loopback, so the connection carried out and the one printed are the same. A run of setup that stopped partway is not an install the wizard offers to go on from, but the schemas and the lingering it made are still this install's: the record it left is read for them, so a run started again over it writes a record that still names them and a later purge still removes them. `nils uninstall --keep-data` keeps the registry where its data is, drops no schema and turns off no lingering, and says under `Keeping` which schemas stay in which database, which a purge on Postgres had never said at all.

- A directory an install left behind is purgeable, and what no record names is named rather than left in silence. `nils uninstall --purge` asked whether the base directory looked like something an install had made, a registry or a desk in it or nothing but the empty folders setup makes, and refused everything else: `/srv/nils will not be removed: it holds neither a registry nor a desk that an install made`, exit 1, on a directory holding the registry's key and nothing else, which is what an install that stopped at the registry leaves. A purge that says it is done and leaves a key on the disk is the worse of the two, so the setup record is now what says a directory is this install's: where a record names it, `--purge` removes it whatever is in it, and the guards that stay are the ones that are not about what it holds, a path that is not absolute, the root of the file system, and the home directory or anything above it. Where no record is left, an uninstall still removes only what a setup leaves in the usual places, and now names what it found in the base directory rather than passing over it: the files under it, the registry's key first, where each of them is, up to ten of them with the rest counted, a line saying that the registry's key is among them, and the command that removes the directory. Nothing there is removed, since without a record nothing says the directory is NILS's.

- Every step `nils setup` takes on a registry dials the database from where that step runs. A container install makes its registry inside the container, so `nils.toml` records the address a container calls this machine by: right for the engine, which runs there, and wrong for setup, which runs out here. Declaring the places opens that registry from the machine, so a container install on a Postgres the site runs stopped at the places, refused by a name only a container resolves. The purge already mapped the address back and the registry's own container steps mapped it forward; the step between them was never reached, because no rootless podman install got that far. Where a step runs is now the one thing that decides the address it dials, and every path asks it: the registry made inside the container dials the container's address, while the registry made on the machine, the check that the database answers, declaring the places, reading the source places back and the drop in a purge dial this machine's. What the registry records is never written over, since the engine goes on running where it was made, and a database on another machine, named the same from both sides, is dialled by the name it was given. A rerun of a pod install now reads its recorded address as this machine's as well, so the pod is given this machine's loopback as the first run gave it. A machine install is unchanged.
- Nothing is registered at a provider until setup knows the desk's configuration can be written. `nils setup` registered the desk at an Authentik, and wrote the client's secret beside the desk, before it wrote the configuration, where the guard that keeps a desk on the store its people are in may refuse. A refusal there left a provider holding a registration nobody uses and a secret file beside a configuration that still named the client before it, and on a rerun it replaced the secret of the registration the desk was using with one for a registration it was never told about. Nothing was lost, but the tidying up was the site's, at a provider setup does not own. What the guard answers is a question about the file on disk and the text that would replace it, and no answer a provider gives names a store, since setup writes the store beside the configuration and keeps the one the file already names, whatever it is; so the question is now asked before anything is registered. Where it refuses, no registration is made and no secret is written, the desk's folder is left as setup found it, and the sentence names the store the people are in, the store the configuration would have named, that nothing was registered at a provider and no secret written, and what to do about it. A registration a rerun finds already at the provider, and its secret, stay as they are. Where it passes, the registration and the secret are made and written in the order they always were, and the guard stands in front of the write as well, for a store that moves while setup works. A print registers nothing, as it always did, and now says a configuration that will not be written rather than an update the run would never make.
- `nils setup --print` says the stops a run would make, and no others. The print listed llama.cpp's unit among the units stopped while their files change whenever the plan named a build, while a run stops it only where it removes an older build, which it does only once it has taken a build this install does not have yet. Every install whose llama.cpp build had not moved, a first install among them, was promised an interruption it never had. The print now asks the folder the same question the run asks: where the build a plan names is here already, or where no other build of this install's sits beside it, llama.cpp is not among the stops. Kvasir and the assistant are listed as they were, since a run takes and builds their source every time. Nothing is reached over the network for this, and a download that cannot be made removes nothing either, which is the one thing the print cannot know without making it: it can still name a stop a run is spared, and it never hides one a run makes.

- A registry directory `nils setup` makes is made 0700, and a directory that was already there keeps the mode it has. The registry holds the key store, which is 0700, and `nils.toml`, which is 0600, and on an install that runs its own Postgres the database's directory as well, so the directory around them came out at whatever the mask allowed, usually 0755, saying less than everything in it. It is now made narrow when setup makes it. The archives, the work in flight and what is exported are made as they always were: they are where an install and the people using it meet, and narrowing them would take away reach rather than tidy anything. Nothing setup finds is touched, mode or owner, whether a site made it, a site declared it as one of its places, or an earlier install left it; and an install whose parts run in containers is unaffected, since each container runs as the account that made the directories it mounts.

## [1.0.0-alpha.35] - 2026-09-17

### Fixed

- An install whose services are the machine's own reaches its registry as the engine's service reaches it, and not as root. `nils setup --system`, run as root, added the registry's key, made the registry, tried a Postgres, declared the places and read the source places back in its own process, as root, while the engine's service reaches the registry as the account it runs as. A Postgres that authenticates peers answers that account and refuses root, so the install was refused a database that was right; a registry on a share that squashes root does not open to root at all, so the install stopped at the registry or its places; and where root could not read the registry's source places, a rerun, `nils setup --update`, a repair, the restart after `nils update --all` and a reapply of the engine took the ones in the setup record instead, without a word, and wrote the engine's unit without every source place added at the desk since, so the engine stopped reading them. Setup now takes every step on the registry as the engine's account, through the engine's own binary under `runuser`, started in `/` with the environment the engine's service runs with, systemd's path, the account's name and home and the language setup runs in, and nothing of root's, so a `NILS_DSN` of root's cannot send a step to another database, root's actor cannot sign the audit rows of the places, and root's `TMPDIR` is not a folder the account cannot enter. The key is added with its passphrase on the step's input and never on a command line; the registry is made by a step of the engine's own that reads a Postgres connection string from its input, since a connection string can carry a password and a command line is every account's to read, and that makes the registry from the settings setup's own process makes it from, so a registry made as the account is the registry setup made in its own process, with the same pseudonym scheme, the same length a code is shown at and the connection string written into `nils.toml` as it was given; a Postgres is tried as the account, at the question and again before the registry is made, with the connection string on the input; the registry's folder and the install's own folders are the account's before it writes into them; the places are declared as the account; and the source places are read back as the account once they are, before any service is written. A machine without `runuser` is named among what the plan lacks, at the question and before anything is placed, rather than stopping the install at the registry with the engine already in place; `--print` is not refused for it. A registry that does not answer the engine's account is said rather than stood in for by the record, with the account and what the registry said: an install stops at its questions with nothing written, `nils setup --update` and a repair stop with nothing changed, `--print` says it and that a run for real stops on it, and a reapply of the engine leaves the engine running as it is; the restart after `nils update --all`, whose parts are replaced by then, starts the services on the recorded places and says that reapplying the engine gives it every source place once the registry answers. A registry at another schema did answer, and is said and gone on from, but for a reapply, which says to restart the engine first, and an install, which refuses a registry ahead of the binary running it. `--print` shows the key, the registry's step and the places as they run, after the `runuser` that runs them, and no connection string among them. An install whose services are an account's own takes these steps as that account, as it always has.

- An install whose services are the machine's own takes Kvasir and the assistant again and follows their releases. `nils setup --system`, run as root, cloned both, installed their packages and built them as root, and handed their folders to the account they run as afterwards, so every later run as root met a checkout that belongs to another account, which git refuses to work in: `nils setup` run again, `nils setup --update` and `nils update --all`, the supervisor's update among them, failed at the part's source with git's `detected dubious ownership`, and neither part followed a release. Setup now takes git, `npm ci` and the build for Kvasir and the assistant as the account they run as, under `runuser`, in the part's folder, with the environment their services run with and nothing of root's, so every file of the checkout and the build is born that account's, on an install, a rerun, `--update` and `nils update --all` alike. Before the clone the part's folder is made the account's, since that account cannot make one in the install's folder, which is root's; a checkout an earlier run left is handed to it whole, so an install that met the refusal is mended by its next run; and a folder that holds something other than a checkout is left as it is. The steps run with the account's own home where the account owns it and may write there, and otherwise with `<dir>/build-cache/<account>`, which setup makes that account's and where npm keeps its cache; no part keeps anything there, so it can be removed whenever nothing is building. `nils update --all` reads the commit a checkout stands at as the same account, so a part whose source did not move is not built again. Where root cannot act as the account, because this machine no longer has it or has no `runuser`, root takes the steps itself, and each git command in the checkout names that one folder safe with `-c safe.directory` on its own command line; nothing is written into root's git settings or the machine's, which would trust the folder for everything run as root from then on. A folder that could not be made the account's is said with the folder and the account named, before a step runs in it. `--print` shows both parts' steps as they run, after the `runuser` that runs them, with the home they build with where that is the build cache. An install whose services are an account's own takes these steps as that account, as it always has.

- Reapplying every part from the supervisor, on an install whose services are the machine's own, writes the services again and starts them. `nils supervise reapply` with no part, which `POST /api/supervise/reapply` runs where no part is named, did the work in the supervisor's own process, as the account the supervisor runs as, where reapplying the engine alone already asked the helper; writing the machine's units is root's, so it said the services were left alone, restarted nothing, and ended as if it had done its work. It now asks the helper as reapplying the engine does, with `sudo -n /usr/local/sbin/nils-manage reapply all`, a command line the helper answers to and its sudoers rule names, and the helper runs `nils supervise reapply --part all` as root: every part's unit written again from the record and started again in the order they start. `reapply` with no other word stays the engine's, so a supervisor older than the helper still asks for what it always asked for, and any other word is refused. The helper and its rule are written by `nils setup` and never by `nils update --all`, so an install brought up to date since its helper was written has a helper that does not know the line. The supervisor reads the helper, which is the machine's to read, before sudo is asked, since sudo would refuse the line and keep the refusal in the machine's log as an attempt at something not allowed, and ends the reapply failed with a sentence saying that nothing was restarted and to run `nils setup` again as root, which writes the helper and its rule again. Run as root, the command does the work itself, as before.

- An install whose services are the machine's own starts the desk, Kvasir, the assistant and llama.cpp where a path their units keep them out of is one root cannot look at. The unit of every part that runs as an account other than the engine's hides the registry, the archives and every folder of DICOM the engine reads with `InaccessiblePaths=-`, and systemd looks at each of those paths as root before the part starts. The `-` forgives only a path that is missing, so a path root is refused at, as archives on a share that squashes root are while the engine's account reads them, stopped each of those parts with `226/NAMESPACE`, and setup had said nothing. Setup now looks at every such path as root once the places and the accounts are settled, before a unit is written, and each path comes to one of three ends. A path root can look at, or one that is missing, keeps its line, and no account is asked. A path root cannot look at that the part's account cannot reach either, asked as that account under `runuser` with one fixed question, needs no hiding: its line is left out of that account's units, with a comment in the unit saying why, and the plan says in a `closed` row, one sentence for each account, which paths are closed to it already. A path root cannot look at that the account can reach, or that the account could not be asked about, cannot be hidden by any unit and is refused with the fix: give root search permission on the folder root may not search, as `chmod 711` does, or close the path to that account, and run `nils setup` again. An install stops on it at its questions with nothing written, and `--print` says it and goes on. `nils setup --update` and a repair look at the paths again, since one can come within an account's reach, or out of root's, after the install, and stop with nothing changed, while `--print` there says it and goes on, as the questions do. The restart after `nils update --all`, whose parts are replaced by then, says which paths are closed and the fix, keeps the line of a path an account can reach, so the services of that account do not start rather than start with it in reach, and starts everything else. At the question, a machine without `runuser`, on which no account can be asked, is named among what the plan lacks rather than refused with a fix it could not follow. A run that is not root, as `--print` may be, looks at nothing, and an install whose services are an account's own hides nothing and is as it was.

- llama.cpp starts where its unit is kept out of the home directories. On an install whose services are the machine's own, with the assistant running as an account other than the engine's and the install outside a home, llama.cpp's unit carries `ProtectHome=yes`, as Kvasir's and the assistant's do. llama.cpp keeps the models it downloads under its account's home unless it is told otherwise, and a unit that cannot even look there stopped at start with `Permission denied` on `~/.cache/huggingface/hub`. The unit now names `LLAMA_CACHE`, `HF_HOME` and `HF_HUB_CACHE` in `<dir>/kvasir/runtime/cache`, as `llama.cpp`, `huggingface` and `huggingface/hub`, inside Kvasir's runtime folder, which is that account's already. The folders are made before the services start, only where Kvasir's runtime folder is there, and handed to the account with the rest of Kvasir's folder; one that cannot be made is said, so the unit that may then not start has its reason beside it. Each assignment is quoted whole, with a quote and a backslash escaped and a percent sign doubled, so a directory with a space or a `%` in it stays one value. These are the folders a drop-in that set the variables by hand used, so removing such a drop-in moves nothing, and while it is there it still wins. Only such a unit changes: where llama.cpp's unit sees a home, as with the services of an account's own, an install that lives in a home, an assistant that runs as the engine's account, or llama.cpp beside parts in containers, its cache stays where it was, since pointing `HF_HOME` elsewhere would hide the models already downloaded and fetch each of them again. `--print` shows the variables in the unit.

- Setup names a library llama.cpp links and the machine lacks before anything starts, and an update, a repair or the restart after `nils update --all` holds llama.cpp alone back where it cannot start. The Linux builds of llama.cpp link `libgomp.so.1`, which a Debian or Ubuntu server may not have. There the loader could not start `llama-server --list-devices`, setup read its silence as a machine without a graphics device and said a model runs on the processor, the install finished, and llama.cpp's unit failed in a loop once the services started. Setup now keeps a table of the libraries these builds link that such a server may lack, each with its package, read from the NEEDED entries of every file in the four Linux archives of b10964: `libgomp.so.1`, from `libgomp1` on Debian and Ubuntu. The C and C++ runtimes and OpenSSL 3 come with those systems, and the Vulkan loader is told of on its own, as before. At the question, on Linux, a library of the table that neither `ldconfig -p`, matched by its whole name, nor the distributions' library folders have is a `missing` row with its package, and where an earlier run unpacked the build already, `ldd` reads what it links and adds each library it names as not found and each version a library here is too old for, such as `libc.so.6 with GLIBC_2.38`; nothing is changed until they are there. Once the build is unpacked, `ldd` reads it again before it is run, and a build that does not run at all is said in the loader's words, its last line, or by how it ended where it said nothing, rather than as a machine without a graphics device. An install stops on either, with the build on record so that `nils uninstall` removes it. `nils setup --update` and a repair say the same and that llama.cpp alone is held back until `nils setup` and a repair can start it: its unit is neither written nor started, a unit an earlier run left is stopped and taken out of the boot, rather than left to fail and be restarted every few seconds on a build the update has replaced and to start into the same loop at the next boot, every other service is written and started without it, since Kvasir copes with a backend that is down, and the setup record still names the build. The unit file is left where it is, and the setup or repair that can start llama.cpp again writes it, enables it and starts it. The restart after `nils update --all` reads the table and `ldd` in the same way, says what the machine lacks, and holds llama.cpp alone back in the same way.

- A rerun keeps the ports the install's own services hold, and each part is stopped only while its own files change. `nils setup` run again on an install that was up moved the ports of its own services: a port counted as this install's own when the setup record named the part, and a run that stopped partway throws its record away, so a rerun after such a run started from no record at all, read the engine's own 8437 as taken, and moved the engine to 8438, writing that port into the units and into everything that had to reach the engine. Nothing was stopped either: the binary, the git checkouts and the npm builds of Kvasir and the assistant were switched under running services, and llama.cpp's older build was removed under the server started from it. Setup now takes the ports of a run that stopped partway from that run's record and asks each of them again what holds it, while the rest of the record is left as what an uninstall would remove, since an install that stopped partway is started again rather than updated or repaired. A port that is taken is asked who holds it, on Linux, from the machine itself: the socket tables name the inodes listening on it, `/proc/<pid>/fd` the processes holding those sockets, and `/proc/<pid>/cgroup` the unit each of them runs under and whether the machine's own manager or an account's runs it. Where that unit is the one this install runs the part under, by name, in the manager this run writes its units to, the port is kept and setup says it is held by that part's own service, which is started again on it; a port anything else holds moves to the next free one, as it always did. In podman and docker a part's port is published by the runtime's own process, so only llama.cpp and the supervisor, which run on the machine beside the containers, are told apart there, and a run that writes no units claims no port as its own. Each part's unit is then stopped just before that part's own files change and started again with the rest: llama.cpp's before the older builds are removed, Kvasir's before its source is taken and built, the assistant's before its, so Kvasir answers while llama.cpp is taken and the assistant while Kvasir is built. A first install stops nothing, a part this install runs no unit for is switched with nothing stopped, and the supervisor is never stopped, since its files are the engine's binary, replaced by a rename, and an update of every part may be a run the supervisor started. A folder adopted in place stops its part too, once the checkout is certain and nothing is refused. `nils update --all` stops a Node part only where its source moves, which is where the commit fetched is not the one the checkout stands at, where either could not be read, or where there is no build to run from, so a part already at its release is not stopped at all; llama.cpp's unit is stopped only where a build is really removed; and a stop counts as a change, so the restart that follows starts the part again. A stop that is refused is said with the call that was refused and the run goes on. `--print` lists the stops a run would make, each call where its unit runs, with what changes beside it.

- A desk keeps the store, the signing key and the client secret file it was given by hand. `store` was one of the keys setup writes, so a rerun replaced a store set by hand with the relative default beside the configuration and the desk started on an empty store it made there: a desk that had people in it had none after, while the plan said setup was keeping what was set by hand. `local.key` went the same way, so a desk that keeps its own people came up with a signing key it had never used and every session it had issued was worthless; and so did `oidc.client_secret_file`, so the desk looked for the provider's secret in a file setup had not written it to. The check that should have caught it read a fixed path beside the configuration rather than the store the configuration names, so it saw the empty default and agreed there was nobody. Setup now keeps those three as the file sets them, alongside every key setup does not write, which was kept already: `store` whatever it names, `local.key` where the file sets one and this run writes a `[local]` table at all, and `oidc.client_secret_file` where the file sets one. A configuration that no longer reads as TOML is written whole again, as before, but its store line is read out of it first and carried into the file that replaces it, so a broken configuration does not move a desk off its people either. Whether a desk has people, which decides whether the wizard offers to add the first one and whether the end of an install says to add one, is now read from the store the configuration names, found the way the desk finds it: a relative path beside the configuration, an absolute path as it stands, and for a desk in a container a path under the folder the desk reads its own files at, which is the same file on this machine. Behind all of that, setup refuses to point a desk at a store that keeps nobody while its people are in another: the configuration is left exactly as it was, nothing is written, and the sentence names the store it would have written and the store the people are in. A store this machine cannot see at all is said the same way. Setup never carries a store from one path to another by itself.

- A part folder that holds a built copy is adopted rather than stopping the install. Setup told a checkout by its `.git` alone and cloned into anything else, and git refuses to clone into a folder that is not empty, so an install whose Kvasir or assistant folder had been laid down by another way of deploying, a built copy with its `state/`, its `kvasir.json` and its `node_modules/` and no repository in it, stopped at that part's source with git's own words, and there was nothing to do but move the folder aside and lose what it held. `nils update --all` took such a folder for a checkout and would have fetched in whatever repository sits in a folder above it. Setup now reads a part's folder three ways: missing or empty, which is cloned into as before; a checkout, which is fetched and checked out as before; and anything else, a copy, which is adopted in place. An adoption makes the repository in the folder, names the part's own repository as its origin and fetches the release tag one commit deep, which touches nothing outside the repository it made, and then reads out the paths that tag tracks. The folder is read along those paths alone, so a large `node_modules` costs nothing, and with lstat, so a link counts as the file it is. A tracked path that would replace something the part keeps is a collision: anything under `state/` or `runtime/`, or `kvasir.json`, `backends-to-add.json`, `assistant.env`, Kvasir's seal and pepper, a key or a store at the folder's top, and also a folder where the release has a file, or a file where the release has a folder. Then the adoption is refused: up to five of them are named with the rest as a count, the repository it made is removed so that the next run does not take the folder for a checkout, the folder is its owner's again, and the sentence says the folder was left as it was and to move those out of it, or the folder aside, and run the command again. Where nothing collides, the release's tag is checked out over the copy, replacing the tracked files a built copy already holds, and everything the release does not track stays as it was, byte for byte; setup says the folder held a copy that was not a checkout and is adopted as a checkout of that release. Where the parts run as an account of their own, the folder itself is lent to that account to make the repository in, the folder alone and not what it holds, and given to it whole only once nothing would be refused. A checkout that fails partway removes the repository too, and a `.git` with no commit that an adoption left behind is removed and the folder adopted again, said in a line; it is told by git finding that `.git` itself rather than one in a folder above, by its HEAD naming no commit, and by the folder holding more than the `.git`, so a repository with no commit and nothing beside it stays the clone that stopped, which a checkout's steps finish, as before. `nils update --all` adopts a copy the same way, with its own command in the sentence, and leaves a folder with nothing in it for `nils setup` and then repair to take again, since an update never clones. `--print` shows an adoption's steps for the services of a machine, as it shows the fetch of a checkout.

## [1.0.0-alpha.34] - 2026-09-17

### Fixed

- An install whose archives are kept at a backup place the site declared no longer stops before it has done anything. `nils setup` counted the archives among the directories an install makes of its own and made them first, as the account running setup, whether they were under the install's directory or at the site's own path. A site keeps its declared places on its own filesystems, and a share can grant the engine's account alone: there the account running setup cannot even look inside the directory, so the install stopped at its first step with permission denied, having written nothing but its record. Archives at a place the site declared are now the site's own, like every other place it declared: made as the place is declared, and never made, looked into or handed to an account by setup. Archives under the install's own directory are made as before.

## [1.0.0-alpha.33] - 2026-09-17

### Fixed

- A desk set up against a provider it is registered at already asks that provider for the entitlements scope where the provider offers it. `nils setup --oidc-issuer URL --oidc-client-id ID`, and the wizard's answer that the desk is registered at a provider already, wrote four scopes into the desk's configuration, `openid`, `profile`, `email` and `offline_access`, whatever the provider was, on the belief that only a registration setup itself makes at Authentik has an entitlements scope. A desk registered at Authentik by hand or by `nils-desk register` has one too, and the desk reads its people's entitlements from it, so after setup had written its configuration that desk stopped asking for them and everyone signed in with none: a person could do only what one of the desk's own groups gave them, and a desk whose access came from the provider's entitlements shut out its readers and its admins alike. Setup now reads the provider's discovery document once, for the signing keys as before and for the scopes it lists, and adds `entitlements` where `scopes_supported` names it. A provider that does not offer the scope is still asked for the four, since a scope a provider does not know fails the sign in.

## [1.0.0-alpha.32] - 2026-09-17

### Added

- An install behind a proxy can be described: `nils setup --origin URL` names the address a browser opens the desk at, where a name and a certificate of the site's answer for it. It is what the desk compares a write against, what it signs the tokens the other parts trust with, and what a person is sent back to after signing in at a provider, so the engine's trust in the desk is derived from the same address. Where the desk itself binds stays a separate answer: this machine's loopback for a proxy running here, and with `--reach network` every address for a proxy on another machine; the loopback stays among the addresses the desk also answers at either way, so a browser on the machine keeps working. An address that is not a scheme and a host is refused before anything is written, in words naming what is wrong with it. The wizard asks for it as a third answer to who may open the desk, and a rerun opens on what is on record. The address is written down in the setup record as `origin`, and an update and a repair write the same address the install did, where before every run stamped `http://<address>:<port>` over it: a desk behind a proxy refused every write by its own cross origin check, dropped `Secure` from its session cookie and sent people back to an address their provider had never heard of, and a correct address set by hand lasted until the next update.

- An install can be a deployment: `nils setup --system` writes the services of the machine rather than of the account that runs setup, unit files in `/etc/systemd/system`, and each part runs as an account of its own, named with `--account engine=nils --account desk=nils-desk` and `nils` for a part not named. The engine's service keeps the capabilities a site's own filesystems ask for, `--capabilities CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE,CAP_DAC_READ_SEARCH`, which is the reason for these services at all: a service of an account's own cannot carry a capability, whatever it is asked for, so this was the kind of unit and not a missing setting. It is asked for outright and never arrived at, and what this machine cannot give is said before a file is written: a container run, a machine with no systemd, a run that is not root, and every account named that this machine has not got, which is the site's own to make and never setup's. What each part reads and writes is given to the account that part runs as, so the desk reads its own configuration and writes its own store, where every file an install writes is otherwise the account that ran setup's alone and a part running as another would start and stop again while the install reported that it had finished. A part that runs as an account other than the engine's is kept out of the home directories, and out of the registry, the archives and the folders of DICOM, which it never reads. The services and the accounts are written down in the setup record, so an update and a repair write the same services rather than falling back to an account's own, and one run by somebody who cannot write them stops before it changes anything. `--print` now shows, for an install on this machine, every unit it would write, where they would go and every call it would make to hand them over, where it showed nothing at all and the guide said otherwise.

- An install can have the places a site actually has: `nils setup --place archives=/data/archives,role=backup,snapshots,protected` names one place, with the role that binds what may be written there, the place that backs it up, and what the site declares about the storage behind it. It is named once for each place, and where any is named those are the places the install declares, at paths of the site's own on whatever filesystems it mounts, in place of the five it had at fixed paths under the install directory with fixed roles, every guarantee false and no way to add a sixth, though `nils place add` has taken all of it from the beginning. `--backup-dir DIR`, `--pack-dir DIR` and `--workers N` set where the engine writes its archives, where it reads its rule packs and how many requests it answers at once, none of which an install could set: the archives were always `<dir>/backups`, and the other two were never given at all, so a real deployment was assembled by hand around the installer instead of made by it. What is wrong with the places named is said before a file is written, with the fix in the sentence: a place with no role or a role that is not one, a registry place that names nothing to back it up or that is marked as read, a name or a directory used twice, a backup that is not a place named, places that keep no registry or keep it somewhere other than where this install does, and archives that no backup place named holds. What each part reads and writes is still handed to the account it runs as, and a site's own filesystems are not: they stay as the site keeps them, which is what the engine's capabilities are for. The places and the settings are written down in the setup record as `[site]`, so an update and a repair declare the same places and write the same settings; a place already declared is left exactly as it is, and one whose directory changed moves rather than being declared twice.

- The engine reads DICOM from the places a site marks, whatever their role: `read`, as in `--place work=/work,role=working,protected,fast,read`, makes a place one of the directories the engine is given, where only a place with the `source` role could be one before. A deployment with ten directories to read had to declare all ten as datasets, which is what `source` means to the registry: it is what a dataset's two trees, the name a question resolves and the verbs that may write there are all decided by, so ten paths carried a role that was not theirs and the registry acted on it. A path now keeps its honest role and is read because the site said it is read. A source place is a dataset and is read whether or not it is marked, so an install that marks nothing reads exactly what it read before, and a place nobody marked is never a directory the desk offers to browse or a queued job can be pointed at.

- The privilege that keeps such an install running is narrow, and written down. Something has to restart the services of the machine and replace the parts' files when a release lands, and both are root's; the supervisor was a plain process of whoever installed it, which on these services meant root. It now runs as an account like every other part, named with `--account supervisor=nils-deploy`, and does that work through a small root owned program `nils setup` writes, `/usr/local/sbin/nils-manage`, with a rule in `/etc/sudoers.d/nils-manage` naming the exact command lines that one account may run it with: `restart` with the name of a part of this install or `all`, `reapply`, and `update`. The program answers to those words alone and takes no path and no command of its caller's, and sudo matches a command and its words exactly, so a call carrying anything else matches no rule and is turned away before the program is reached. The other answer, sudo for the account the engine runs as, is the one not taken: that account reads every scan in the registry, and sudo for it would make a compromise of the engine a compromise of the machine. So the account is required and is nobody else's: `--system` without `--account supervisor=NAME`, or with one a part already runs as, is refused at the question before anything is written, and there is no falling back to the default account for it.
