The rule is read by `visudo` before it is put in place, since a sudoers file sudo cannot parse shuts an operator out of root, and where visudo refuses it nothing is written and the install stops with what visudo said; a machine without `sudo` or `visudo` is named among what the plan lacks, before anything is placed. `nils supervise restart`, `nils supervise reapply` and `nils update --all` all go through it where the caller is not root, and do the work themselves where it is. The account, the program and the rule are written down in the setup record as `[helper]`, so an update and a repair lay down the same arrangement and `nils uninstall` takes it away again, the rule before the program it named. An install whose services are the account's own has none of this and grows none of it.

### Fixed

- An install on a machine nobody is logged in to says so before it writes anything, rather than writing every file and reporting at the end that nothing started. The account is lingered before the units are handed to systemd instead of after them, since lingering is what gives an account nobody is logged in as a user manager at all; the account lingered is the one running setup, read from the system rather than from `$USER`, which names another account under `pct exec` and in other contexts nobody logged in to; and whether services can be kept running here is settled by asking the user manager itself, where `systemctl --user --version` was asked before and answers from the binary alone, session or none, so setup believed it could manage services it could not. A `systemctl --user daemon-reload` that is refused now stops the install with what was refused named and the fix in the same sentence, where its status was swallowed; `--service` on such a machine is refused at the question rather than at the end; and a docker install, whose containers its own daemon brings back, no longer wants a session of this account at all.

## [1.0.0-alpha.31] - 2026-09-17

### Added

- The HTTP API contract is version 6. It is published as `contracts/openapi/v6/`, a new directory beside version 5, and `contracts/openapi/v5/` is untouched: version 6 is version 5 with the one door below added and nothing else changed. A client pinned to contract 5 is unaffected by this release and has nothing to change.
- The engine serves the tag policy it owns (HTTP API contract version 6, decision record 28). `GET /api/pseudonymize/tags` answers what the pseudonymiser does to the standard elements it acts on: the four categories it removes tag for tag, patient, trial, provider and institution, and the hundred elements in them, each written `gggg,eeee` as a dataset's own lists are written, with its category and what becomes of it, `removed`, or `replaced` for the age, computed from the birth date and the study date and put in place of whatever was there, or `kept` for a covariate; the element the subject's code is written into, replaced rather than removed and in no category, so that it is never offered to keep; the two that are never removed, and why; and the three covariates a dataset keeps unless it opts out, with the switch that opts out named. Never the times, which are a release's and not the pseudonymiser's. The answer carries numbers, categories and fates and no tag names, which the engine has never held: the policy is the engine's and the words a reader needs belong to whatever shows the list. A dataset's own `keep` and `remove` lists are that dataset's and stay where they are, with the dataset. Any grant opens the door, which reads the constants of the binary and says nothing of the registry. A client pinned to contract 5 meets the engine it already knew.

## [1.0.0-alpha.30] - 2026-09-16

No change of its own. Released beside the desk's 1.0.0-alpha.30 so the two stay in step: a container install names both images with the engine's version. The change of this release is the desk's, record 27, where the pages say a value first and keep their explanations behind a disclosure, a dataset is added by browsing the engine's own folders, the tags the pseudonymiser removes are shown and chosen among, a map states its columns and rehearses before filing, and Rules walks every axis.

## [1.0.0-alpha.29] - 2026-09-16

### Added

- A source place is a dataset (HTTP API contract version 5). It declares what arrives in it, `identified`, `deidentified` or `coded`; its two trees, `derivatives/dcm-original`, the originals the pseudonymiser alone reads, and `derivatives/dcm-anon`, the pseudonymised tree the registry reads, or the folder itself where there is no such layout, which is how every place declared before keeps working; the identity rule its files are read under, as `nils digest --identity-rule` reads a file, which a digest of the dataset takes when it names none of its own; what an identifier the linkage store does not know does, `hold` or `code`; the cohort every digest of it feeds; and its tag lists, `keep_demographics`, `remove` and `keep`. What becomes of the originals it does not declare: `originals_kept` and `originals_vault` are written by the acts on them alone, and a body naming either at those doors is refused in words naming the act that sets it. `POST /api/places` and `PUT /api/places/{id}` take the rest beside the place's own fields, under `data:work` beside `places:work`, which the capabilities and the policy rows say; `nils place add` and `nils place set` take them as `--arrives`, `--identity FILE`, `--unmapped`, `--cohort` or `--no-cohort`, `--keep-demographics` or `--no-keep-demographics`, `--remove TAG`, `--keep TAG` and `--move-into-anon`, and `nils place list` shows what arrives and the trees. Declaring a source place looks at its folder: a v0 cohort folder's `derivatives/dcm-raw` is renamed `derivatives/dcm-anon` and nothing else is rewritten; an identified folder's loose entries are moved into the originals and an empty pseudonymised tree is made beside them; a de-identified or coded folder's are moved into the pseudonymised tree when asked with `move_into_anon`. Every move is a rename inside the folder, top-level entries only, and the answer carries `layout` with a v0 folder's counts. A folder that is a tree of another dataset is refused. `POST /api/ingest/look` answers `layout` for a folder before it is declared. `GET /api/sources` and `GET /api/places` show the dataset, each tree with its path and the files, bytes and last write the last probe counted, refreshed by `?probe=1` and never by a walk on every call, and what the pseudonymiser holds for want of a map, zero until there is one; each recent digest carries `pseudonymised`, null until a pseudonymise step precedes it. `@name` now resolves to the dataset's pseudonymised tree wherever a location is named, in a queued command, the picker's doors, `nils ingest probe` and `nils digest`, and `@name/originals` to its originals, which a digest is refused since the registry never points at an identified file; `nils place bindings` shows both. The registry's schema is at version 44 after this record's five migrations and the one lab 26d asked for; the first, 39, adds `place.dataset` and the `pseudonym_file` table the pseudonymiser records every file of the originals in.
- The pseudonymiser (record 26). `nils pseudonymize @dataset` rewrites an identified dataset's originals into its pseudonymised tree: the code in `PatientID`, resolved through the dataset's identity rule and the linkage store exactly as the digest resolves it; the patient, provider, trial and institution groups removed tag for tag; `PatientAge` written; sex, weight and size kept unless the dataset opts out; the dataset's `remove` and `keep` lists applied, keep winning; every date and UID kept; the private elements dropped except what the pack's release list names; the overlays and curves dropped; the pixel bytes copied from the source untouched. The copies land under `<code>/<StudyDate>-<8 hex of the study UID>/<series>/<instance>.dcm`, never under the path they came in on, written `.part` and renamed, hashed on the way, and the original hashed in that same pass, from its first byte to its last, its digest recorded beside the copy's so that what a purge destroys can be proved by its content; the tree is synced once per run. A run resumes by size and modification time (`pseudonym_file`, one row per file, with its directory) and then proves what those brought back on both sides, the copy against the digest that was recorded and the original against its own: it writes a changed source again over its own place, writes a copy again where it has gone or no longer hashes to what was recorded, and writes again an original whose bytes moved under a modification time that did not, or whose row is from before the original's digest was recorded, so that a tree whose copies were corrupted, and originals a purge cannot prove, are both mended by running the dataset again. It reads only the held rows a map released or a person coded anyway with `--held`, rehearses with `--dry-run`, and stops at its next heartbeat on a cancel with what was written kept. A file the linkage store does not know is held with its shape, keyed lookup and sealed identifier when the dataset says `hold`, and one review item `identity.unmapped` per dataset and shape, counts and the shape only, is opened by the runs and superseded by the run that holds nothing under it; when it says `code`, or a person asked for the file to be coded anyway, the subject is made and marked `provisional`, with one `identity.provisional` item per subject. The run is a batch of kind `pseudonymize`, its report (`files`, `subjects`, `tags_removed`, `private_removed`, `bytes`, `seconds`) the batch's counts and the job's result, and the job's progress every heartbeat. The verb takes a dataset by its name alone and refuses one read in place. A digest of an identified dataset's tree reads the codes verbatim under the `subject-code` type, which every linkage store now holds, so a subject the pseudonymiser made is found by its code. `POST /api/jobs` queues `pseudonymize` under `data:work` at detail sensitive. Migration 40 adds `ingest_batch.kind`, `subject.provisional` and `pseudonym_file.dir`.
- The chain (record 26 §7). `POST /api/jobs` takes `then: [command, ...]`, the command lines a worker queues one after another when the job before ends done, under the principal, grants and detail recorded on the first job; a step the recorded grants do not reach ends the chain and the job's result says which step and why (`chain_stopped`). A job answers `then` and `chain {before, after}` on `GET /api/jobs/{id}`, in `GET /api/jobs`, in the event stream and in `nils jobs show --json`. `bring-in @dataset [--name N] [--pack P]`, at the door and as `nils bring-in`, queues the thread of a dataset: pseudonymise, then digest, fingerprint and classify for an identified dataset, or the digest and the rest for any other, the digest sharing the pseudonymise step's name. A job queued at a door records the caller's grants.
- The batch is the thread (record 26 §14). `GET /api/batches/{id}` answers `stages {pseudonymised, walked, digested, classified, reviewed}`, read off the batch and the one it shares a name with on the other side of the thread, the jobs over its stacks and the questions on them; `GET /api/batches` and `nils status` say each batch's `kind`; `GET /api/timeline/batch/{id}` exists; `GET /api/sources` fills each recent digest's `pseudonymised` from the step before it, lists no pseudonymise step as a digest, and carries `rates {pseudonymize, digest}`, the files per second of the last run of each on this machine.
- What becomes of a dataset's originals is acted on and not only declared (record 26). `POST /api/places/{id}/originals {do, into, why}` vaults a dataset's originals into another place or purges them, as a job of kind `originals` under `data:work` at detail sensitive; `GET /api/places/{id}/originals` answers what the act would do without doing it, `{files, bytes, verified, unverified, copy_unverified, changed, unproved, no_copy, held, ready, why, forecast}`, under `data:see`; and `nils place originals <dataset> --vault --into PLACE --why TEXT` or `--purge --why TEXT` does the same at the keyboard, printing what the door answers before it acts and with neither flag printing only that. A vault moves the originals under `originals/<dataset>` in a backup place, the one role the engine never reads for data: a rename where the destination is on the same filesystem, and otherwise a copy whose digest is compared with the source's before the source is removed, so no file is ever removed whose copy did not verify. A purge is refused while any file of the dataset waits for a map, since those originals are the only copy of those people's scans, and unless every original is verified: its copy in the pseudonymised tree at the size and the digest that were recorded, hashed again now, **and** the original itself still the file that copy was made from, at the size and the modification time the row recorded. A file changed after its copy was written passes the copy's half alone, and purging it would destroy the only version of it that exists, so the two halves are asked together; a modification time that moved innocently is refused too, and the refusal says what to do. The unverified are counted and said apart, because their cures differ: a copy that is missing or is no longer what was recorded, an original that moved on since it was copied (both mended by pseudonymising the dataset again, which now writes either again), and a file the reader refused, which is not DICOM, has no copy and never will, and is left for a person to move out of the tree rather than deleted. A purge proves each original by its content, and reads every one of them to do it: immediately before a file is removed the original is opened, measured and hashed whole, and it is that reading that decides, never the walk's listing of the directory, which may be many files old by the time the file's turn comes, and never a size and a modification time alone, which anything may set. So a file an rsync or a re-export writes under the running walk is left rather than destroyed, and so is one changed in place whose modification time was then put back to the recorded value, which passed as verified before. A row written before the original's digest was recorded can be proved by nothing and is refused as `unproved`, in words naming the run of the pseudonymiser that reads the original and records what it hashes to; that run notices both of these where a resume by size and modification time alone would not. The survey and the door stay cheap, on the rows and on each original's size and modification time, and carry `forecast`, a sentence saying that they are a forecast and that the purge proves each file as it reaches it, so that nobody is told `ready` and then met by a refusal with nothing to explain it. The refusals run in the survey and at the door before a job is queued, so a purge that may not run is refused in words rather than queued and failed. Migration 44 adds `pseudonym_file.original_digest`. A dataset read in place is refused outright, having nothing to verify against. Both acts resume, stop at a heartbeat on a cancel with what was done kept and the job reading `cancelled`, and are audited as `originals.vault` and `originals.purge` with their counts and the reason, which moves the epoch. A dataset now carries `originals_vault`, the name of the place a vault put its originals in, beside `originals_kept`, and the act sets both when its job succeeds; until then the dataset reads `kept`. Neither act touches the pseudonymised tree, the registry's rows or the linkage store.
- A release plan names tags to keep and to remove beside its categories, which is how the pseudonymiser keeps the demographics and applies a dataset's lists; the release itself names none.
- The reader frames a file for rewriting, `nils_dicom::read_framed`: the header as before, and where the pixel data begins and ends, found by walking the bytes.
- Cohorts have doors, and a dataset feeds one (record 26, sections 8 to 11). A digest of a dataset whose `cohort` names one opens a membership interval for every subject it creates, and for one it meets whose membership is not open, with `source = digest` and the batch; the cohort is made on first use, owned by the job's principal and described as fed by the dataset, audited as `cohort.create` and `cohort.join` with the counts, and the digest's report and the batch's record carry `joined {cohort, subjects, met, created}`, or the refusal when the name could not be a cohort's. `GET /api/cohorts` lists every cohort with its subjects, sessions and stacks, the datasets feeding it, where it came from (a source, a promotion, a hand or an import), the open review items about its members, the releases naming it and whether it is retired; `GET /api/cohorts/{name}` adds the joins and removals grouped by batch, handle or hand act, the sources holding the members and the releases; `POST /api/cohorts` makes one and refuses a name a cohort or a selection holds; `PUT /api/cohorts/{name}` renames, keeping the id and what recorded the old name, re-owns, describes, retires or brings back; `POST /api/cohorts/{name}/members` adds and removes by code with a reason, and refuses a code the registry does not hold before anything is written, naming the codes. Reading is `data:see`, writing `data:work`, and every act is audited and moves the epoch. `nils clinical cohort list|show|make|rename|set|retire|add|remove` do the same. A retired cohort keeps its members and its history and leaves `GET /api/summary` and the ask's catalog. `nils ask promote` and `POST /api/ask/handles/{id}/promote` take a complete handle at session or stack grain too, filing the distinct subjects of its rows with the grain and the row count on the intervals. `GET /api/review?cohort=` keeps the items whose subject holds an open membership, `GET /api/review/summary?cohort=` counts the open items by kind and by cohort with the items about no member under `none`, and `GET /api/quarantine?cohort=` narrows to the batches that fed the cohort. `GET /api/explain/{stack}` answers what `nils explain` reads, from one reading both share: each axis with its value, the pack's label, confidence and tier, the evidence under it and the decision that set it, with the why. Migration 41 adds `cohort.retired_at`, `cohort_member.batch_id` and `release.policies`.
- A release says what it did with its sessions (HTTP API contract version 5). `GET /api/releases` answers `sessions`, how many sessions the release holds, counted as the distinct subject and session pairs its own stacks were written under and null where it wrote none or a later version has written over all of them; `session_scheme`, the scheme that named them, so a caller can say whether they are labelled by their date, by months or in order; and `session_naming`, the sentence naming the dataset whose declared shift had them numbered in date order instead of labelled the way the scheme asked (section 4.3), which the release row now records beside the scheme where it only ever reached the release job's result. `nils release --history --json` answers that document too, where it printed the human history whatever was asked of it. Migration 43 adds `release.session_naming`, null on every row written before it.
- A release reads each dataset's leaving policy (record 26, section 13). When neither `--dates` nor `--uids` is given, the policy of each file comes from the dataset holding it, the `handling.on_release` of the source place whose pseudonymised tree holds the file's source root, and a file under no dataset leaves under the run's defaults; a release spanning datasets applies each dataset's to its own files, refuses the shift-with-preserved-UIDs combination under the dataset's name, keeps one date offset per subject and one UID remapping for the run, and lets the strictest policy in play rule the tree's one clinical export. The flags override every dataset's for the run, and the release row says so in `policy.from`; the row records `policies: [{dataset, dates, uids, from}]` beside `policy`, the report, `GET /api/releases` and `nils release` carry them. `--dataset NAME` and `datasets` on `POST /api/releases` select the files under a dataset's tree, and the door takes `uids`.
- The identifier map in any shape (record 26). `nils linkage import` learns the roles of a CSV's columns with `--column HEADER=identifier:<type>`, `--column HEADER=canonical:<type>` (the identifier that stands for the person, from which the subject's code derives under the registry's scheme and key, exactly as a digest derives it), `--column HEADER=code` and `--column HEADER=ignore`; the old `--id-type`, `--id-column` and `--code-column` still work and mean the same, and v0's `PatientID,subject_code` file imports as it is. Several identifier columns in one row are several identifiers of one subject; an empty cell is skipped; a row resolves to one subject by its code, by its canonical identifier, or by an identifier already known, and adds the rest. The map is validated whole and then applied, and reports what it will do before writing: subjects named, known and new; identifiers filed, known and new; held files released; subjects merged; and the conflicts, on which nothing is written. `--dry-run` prints the report and writes nothing, `--make-types` makes the types the columns name, `--json` prints the report, and the report is the job's result. The identifiers never appear in the report or the audit. `POST /api/linkage/imports` takes the same map as JSON, answers a dry run at once and queues a `linkage import` job from a file it writes under the registry home (mode 600, removed when the job ends) otherwise; more than 100,000 rows in one call are refused with 413. A map that names an identifier a held file carries releases the file for the next pseudonymise.
- A later link merges. `nils linkage merge <canonical> <alias> --why TEXT`, `POST /api/linkage/merge` (a `linkage merge` job) and `nils linkage link`, which records the link as before and then merges, re-point every row of the alias that carries a subject to the canonical one in one transaction (studies, series, fingerprints, memberships, clinical rows, picks, handover subjects, handle and list members, decisions at subject scope; the session cache of both is dropped for the next rebuild), close a membership of the alias that duplicates an open one of the canonical with `left_by = merge`, move the alias's identities, file the alias's code on the canonical subject as an identifier of the new type `subject-code`, leave the alias row marked `merged_into` and `merged_at` (migration 42, which also seeds the `subject-code` type) and out of every listing and count, close its provisional review item, and record `subject.merge` in the audit with both codes, which moves the epoch. An import whose row maps an identifier of one existing subject to the canonical identifier of another merges the first into the second, and says so first.
- Identifier types have doors: `GET /api/linkage/types` lists each type with how many identifiers and subjects it holds, `POST /api/linkage/types {name, description}` makes one, once by name; `nils linkage types` prints the same. The held files of a dataset have doors against the pseudonymiser's table: `GET /api/linkage/held?place=` answers the shapes with counts, `POST /api/linkage/held/code {place}` marks them to be coded anyway on the next run, and `POST /api/linkage/held/reveal {place}` answers the identifiers themselves by shape, one `read_audit` row per identifier. Reading identifiers (the map applied, the reveal, the merge) needs `data:work` at detail sensitive; the dry run's report and the held list hold no identifier and need `data:work` and `data:see`.
- Every axis value's word list is a site's to amend through an overlay (pack contract 5). An overlay carries `lists` beside its `buckets`, each list named `axis.value` by the value's identity or its label, with the words it adds and removes; the merge is the buckets' (the pack's words, then the additions deduplicated case-insensitively with the first spelling kept, then the removals), applied when the pack loads and never written into it. A value the axis tries by a flag alone gains a word tier from a list. A value no word of the pack's reaches (a route sets it, or it is the default), a value or an axis the pack does not have, and anything but words (a flag, a physics window, a threshold, the order, or the keys of a value) are refused, and the refusal says what stays the pack's. The overlay document has a schema of its own, `contracts/pack/v5/overlay.schema.json`. An engine at contract 5 loads a contract-4 pack unchanged; the MRI pack stays at contract 4. `POST /api/classify/try` and `POST /api/overlays` take `lists`; `nils overlay show` prints them and `nils overlay export` writes them; `nils classify --overlay` applies them; adoption re-sorts the scope as before; `overlay_unused` counts a list's terms as it counts a bucket's.
- `GET /api/packs/{name}` and `nils pack show --json` answer every axis in the order it is decided with its values in the order they are tried: each value's name, label and family, whether the axis tries it, its keywords after any overlay, the bucket they came from, the list an overlay amends it by, the detection clauses in words (the exclusive flag, the alternative flags, the flag combinations, the physics windows as written with their confidence and why), and the terms the registry's adopted overlays put on it. Beside the axes: the flags count, the review thresholds, every amendable list, the site's terms per list and the adopted overlays. `GET /api/packs` says how many `lists` and `buckets` each pack opens. `nils pack show` prints the count of lists.
- `GET /api/classify/signals` answers `by_value`: per axis and value, how many stacks were decided to it and by which kind of clause (`by_flag`, `by_word`, `by_physics`), how many a person is still asked about (`unsure`), the words shadowed on the way to it and the words the rule cited on stacks a person then decided otherwise (`overrode_with`); and `origins`, the manufacturers, models and stations of the scope with their counts. Bounded as the signals are.

### Changed

- The digest's identity resolution is one `Resolver` the writer and the pseudonymiser share; a code read verbatim is no longer a display-code collision with the subject whose code it is, whatever identifier that code was derived from.
- A promotion is a cohort act, so `POST /api/ask/handles/{id}/promote`, the queued `ask promote` and its cancel need `data:work` where they needed `release:work`, and a promotion into a retired cohort or under a selection's name is refused.
- `nils explain --json` prints the document the explain door answers: the evidence sits under each axis with the decision that set it, where it was one list beside the axes; the text is as before.
- A source place declared with nothing said about it arrives de-identified, where its handling read as identified; `handling.arrives` mirrors the dataset's `arrives` and takes `coded` too.
- `nils setup` names every graphics card the machine has and the memory of them all, as in `2 × NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000, 191 GB`, where it named only the first card nvidia-smi listed. The supervisor's install report carries `machine.cards`, each card with its name and memory, beside `machine.card`, which is the card with the most memory and what the advice reads.
- The install takes the assistant 1.0.0-alpha.25, whose stations read a dataset and tune any word list, and Kvasir 1.0.0-alpha.7 as before.

### Fixed

- A queued job whose verb was asked to stop reads `cancelled` where it read `failed`. The worker took the verb's exit status alone, and a verb that stops exits 130, so a digest, a release or an act on a dataset's originals that a person stopped themselves was recorded as having failed and every page said so; what the verb printed last is kept as the row's word for it either way.
- A merge keeps a cohort membership whole, so `nils linkage merge`, `POST /api/linkage/merge` and an import whose row merges two subjects no longer end on a unique constraint over `cohort_member` when alias and canonical joined one cohort in one digest, which is the common case. An identifier map applies as one transaction on each store, so a map that is refused halfway leaves no subject behind it.
- An identifier map releases a held file whatever type it names the value under, and its report says how many files each identifier released. A map that named a held value under another type released nothing and said nothing about it.
- A job that fails under the worker records what the verb printed, not its exit status alone, so a card on Pipelines says why it stopped.
- A pseudonymise dry run queued at the door records its report as the job's result, where the report existed only on the command line's own output.
- `POST /api/ask/run` with `keep` and a name a kept handle holds already is refused in words, where it ended on a unique constraint.
- A job keeps the command line it was queued with under `args.queued`, `argv` says what ran, and the queue's worker is no job the doors list.
- The pseudonymiser leaves an original the pseudonymised tree holds already as it is, so a v0 cohort folder's files are not written a second time beside v0's own, and `bring-in` on such a dataset digests the tree it has before it pseudonymises anything.
- A pseudonymised tree's codes are read verbatim whatever their length, so a v0 cohort folder's sixteen hex characters are read as the codes they are: with v0's map filed, a person keeps the code they had and is one subject and not two. A value no subject holds stands as its own code where it has the shape this registry makes, and is otherwise an identifier a code is derived from.
- A release whose dates move *because a dataset declares it* numbers its sessions in date order instead of refusing to run: where neither `--dates` nor `--uids` is given and a dataset's `on_release` shifts or truncates dates under a session scheme that labels by the date, the release row records the scheme that named them and the report says why it is not the scheme that was asked for. A declared policy that could never be released would be a declaration nobody could use, and an ordinal label leaks no date. Where the run's own `--dates` moves them, the same combination is still refused and no tree is written, and the refusal now names the dataset's declaration as the case resolved instead. A months or ordinal scheme named by the caller stands as it is, and dates that move with UIDs preserved is refused either way.
- A digest of a dataset read in place reads what it says of an identifier the linkage store does not know. Where the dataset says `hold`, the file is not filed: its `source_file` row is quarantined under `identity.unmapped` with the shape of the identifier, no subject is made for it, and one review item per dataset and shape says how many, opened and closed as the pseudonymiser's are; a held file is read again by the next digest, so a map filed since releases it. Where it says `code`, the subject is made and marked provisional, as the pseudonymiser marks it.
- `POST /api/ingest/look` takes `path`, the absolute path of a folder under no ingest location, which is how the desk looks at a folder before a dataset is declared on it; the look is the same one and bounded the same way, and the answer names no location.
- A dataset's subjects are the subjects whose files its tree holds, whoever made them, on `GET /api/sources` and on a batch's digested stage. They were the subjects a digest created, so a dataset whose subjects an identifier map made read as none.
- `GET /api/sources` takes each rate from the last run of that step that read or wrote at least a hundred files, else the largest run there was, and says how many files it was measured over: `rates` carries `{files_per_s, files}` per step. A resume run of ten files reported eighty thousand files a second.
- Each recent digest on `GET /api/sources` names the jobs of its thread by stage under `chain`, where it carried none.
- A cohort counts the sessions of its members out of the session cache as it stands, and answers none where nobody has built it; every cohort answered zero. `GET /api/summary` and `GET /api/sources` say the window the cache was built under.
- A digest of a dataset read in place asks about the people it codes. Where the dataset says `code`, or a person has asked for a held file to be coded anyway, the subject made from an identifier no map named opens one `identity.provisional` review item per subject, with the files under it and the shape of the identifier and never a value, exactly as the pseudonymiser opens one. The subject was marked provisional in the registry and nothing on Review said those people had been coded without a map.
- A digest's `identity.unmapped` items are answered by the run that holds nothing under them any more, as the pseudonymiser's are, so a dataset whose files have since been coded no longer counts them as waiting for a map. The items stayed open whatever a later run did, and Review counted files as held that were not.
- What a digest holds waits for a map where the pseudonymiser's held files wait. A file held by a digest of a dataset read in place is recorded in `pseudonym_file` with the shape of its identifier, the keyed lookup and the identifier sealed, so `GET /api/linkage/held`, `POST /api/linkage/held/code`, `POST /api/linkage/held/reveal` and the `held` count on `GET /api/sources` answer for such a dataset, which showed nothing at all while its files sat quarantined. An identifier map that names one of those identifiers releases its files as it releases the pseudonymiser's, under another type as well as its own, and the next digest reads them again and files them; a file a person has had coded anyway is coded by the next digest and its subject marked provisional. A file the next run files waits for nothing any more and its row goes, and the held rows of a dataset that arrives identified stay the pseudonymiser's own. A file both tables record waits once and not twice, so what a refusal about the originals says is the count the sources door and `GET /api/linkage/held` answer.
- A job queued at a door takes the engine's workers where the caller named none, so a digest or a pseudonymise run beside the doors by `nils serve --worker` runs with the `--workers` the engine was started with rather than one per core. A command line that names `--workers` keeps what it asked for, a verb with no such flag is left alone, and a worker started by hand with `nils jobs work` is unchanged.
- A release says how many stacks the layout had no place for. The report carries `left_out` beside `routes` and `nowhere`, `nils release` prints it, a BIDS run says that its files are NIfTI and its sidecars with DICOM under `sourcedata/`, and the tree's README names the count and the reasons. A BIDS tree of a cohort holds fewer files than a descriptive one for those two reasons, and neither was written down anywhere a person would look. The report is also the release job's result now, so a release queued at a door says what it wrote and what it left out, where the counts reached the command line alone.

## [1.0.0-alpha.28] - 2026-09-15

### Changed

- The engine checks grants and detail in place of the role ladder (suite contract version 2, HTTP API contract version 4, MCP contract version 2). A caller holds a set of grants, each naming a page and how far it goes there (`see`, or `work`, which includes see; the assistant has `use`), and a detail, `plain`, `quasi` or `sensitive`, for how much of a record it sees. A token's `grants` and `detail` claims are taken as they are. `--role GROUP=X` and `--token TOKEN=user@node:LIST` take grants beside the ladder names, which stand for their sets: reader is `query:work`, `data:see` and plain; reviewer adds `review:work`, `pipelines:see` and quasi; operator adds `data:work`, `release:work`, `pipelines:work`, `places:work`, `install:see`, `kvasir:see`, `assistant-settings:see` and sensitive; admin is every grant but `assistant:use`; `assist` is `assistant:use`. A caller left with no grant is refused with 403. Every door needs its grant, which its policy row and a refusal name; `POST /api/jobs` checks the grant of each verb, a cancel the grant of the job's verb, and adopting an overlay needs `review:work` and `data:work`. Saving a selection, uploading a list and starting from one need detail quasi, the viewer's pixels quasi and burned-in annotation sensitive, and projecting raw identifiers sensitive. `X-Nils-Ceiling` keeps the grants of its step's set and `assistant:use` and lowers detail to the step's. The capabilities carry `grants`, `detail` and, for one release, `roles` as the ladder steps up to the detail; the MCP door's metadata names the grants as its scopes. A subject that already holds `@` is taken as the principal only from a trust entry that keeps subjects, `--oidc-trust ...,keep_subject=true`, as the desk's own entry is to; any other entry qualifies every subject by the issuer's host, as before.
- Setup, where people sign in through a provider, has the engine and Kvasir trust the tokens the desk signs beside the provider's, marks the desk's trust entry as keeping its subjects, and names the provider's groups claim in the desk's configuration; `nils update --all` mends Kvasir's trust on an install from before. The install takes Kvasir 1.0.0-alpha.7 and the assistant 1.0.0-alpha.24, which read grants.

### Fixed

- `NILS_TOKENS` keeps an entry's own list: `T1=bo@lab:reader,kvasir:see` is one entry, as `--token` takes it, since a piece that holds no `=` belongs to the entry before it. The variable was split at every comma, so an entry listing more than one name stopped `nils serve` at start.
- A job queued through `POST /api/jobs` records the caller's detail, and a job that recorded none runs as plain; an `ask run` queued there ran with every class. Of the ask verbs that door queues `ask run` and `ask promote`, and no other.
- `GET /api/review`, `GET /api/review/{id}`, `POST /api/ask/values` and `GET /api/instances/*` need what their handlers needed, the stricter of the two, now that each policy row is read from the door table.

## [1.0.0-alpha.27] - 2026-09-15

### Fixed

- `nils update --all` takes the newest `nils` first and hands it the rest of the update, so every part moves to the versions the newest release pins. It updated the parts first with the binary it had, and replaced that binary last, so an update from 1.0.0-alpha.24 built Kvasir at 1.0.0-alpha.4 and never installed llama.cpp, which that version did not know. The update now says that no setup is recorded before it fetches anything.

## [1.0.0-alpha.26] - 2026-09-14

### Fixed

- `nils setup` pins Kvasir 1.0.0-alpha.6, which keeps a stream open while a model reads a long prompt. On the processor, llama.cpp read a prompt of the assistant's for seven minutes before its first token, the answer stayed silent, and a question to the assistant failed as "terminated" with nothing written.

## [1.0.0-alpha.25] - 2026-09-14

### Added

- `nils setup` installs llama.cpp's server wherever it installs the assistant, so a model Kvasir downloads can be started from Kvasir without a model server of your own. It takes llama.cpp build b10964 for this machine: on Linux the Vulkan build where there is a graphics card or a render node and the CPU build otherwise, on macOS the build for its processor. The archive is checked against the sha256 this version pins and unpacked into `<dir>/llama.cpp/`, and setup names the devices llama.cpp runs a model on. The plan says when a Linux machine has no Vulkan loader, since a model then runs on the processor until one is installed. llama.cpp runs as `nils-llama.service` on this machine whichever runtime the parts use (a launchd agent on macOS), started before Kvasir, in router mode with no model loaded and at most one at a time. It listens only where Kvasir reaches it (127.0.0.1:7110 by default, docker's bridge for a docker install), with a key only Kvasir and llama.cpp read, and keeps its presets, key and log in `<dir>/kvasir/runtime/`. `kvasir.json` names it under `local.runtime`, and where Kvasir runs in a container, `hostAlias` names how Kvasir reaches this machine's own loopback. An archive that cannot be downloaded, or whose sha256 is not the pinned one, stops an install; `NILS_SETUP_LLAMA_RELEASES` names a mirror, held to the same digests. `nils update --all` takes the build a new version pins, or takes it for an install from before that has the assistant, and restarts it. `nils uninstall` removes it.

### Fixed

- `nils uninstall` stops the services it removes from this machine on a docker install too. It removed the supervisor's unit there and left the supervisor running.

## [1.0.0-alpha.24] - 2026-09-14

Released beside nils-desk 1.0.0-alpha.24 so the two stay in step. The engine itself is unchanged since 1.0.0-alpha.23.

## [1.0.0-alpha.23] - 2026-09-14

Released beside nils-desk 1.0.0-alpha.23 so the two stay in step, and with Kvasir 1.0.0-alpha.4 and nils-assistant 1.0.0-alpha.23, which `nils setup` now takes at those tags.

### Added

- `POST /api/ingest/folders` and `POST /api/ingest/look` let an operator choose what to digest from the engine's own ingest locations, where no supervisor answers. The first lists the locations, each with the place that holds it, or a page of the folders inside a folder named as `@root/relative`, filtered by name and paged after a name, at most 1,000 a page, each folder with whether it can be opened and the place that holds it. A folder's names are kept for a minute while it does not change, so a folder of a hundred thousand folders is read once for all its pages. The second says what a few folders hold: a sample of sixteen files each, read breadth first and sniffed for DICOM with their modalities and scanners, within a budget the folders share, and a folder the budget did not reach says so, to be asked for again. Nothing outside the ingest locations is listed: a parent step, a leading slash and a link that leaves its location are refused, links inside are left out as the digest leaves them out, and a listing reads the disk for at most five seconds.
- `nils setup` offers your ChatGPT subscription as what the assistant talks to, where nobody signs in. Once Kvasir runs, setup shows a link and a code, waits while you sign in with ChatGPT, and sends the assistant's purposes that read no rows of the archive to the subscription; the prompt then leaves your systems. The purposes that read rows stay closed to it until an admin opens them in the desk's settings, or stay on a local model that serves them. A sign-in that fails or outlives its code stops an install, and running `nils setup` again and keeping the subscription signs in. With nobody at the terminal no sign-in is started. Where people sign in, a subscription is each person's own, signed in from the desk, and setup offers none.

### Changed

- `nils setup` sets up Kvasir, the model gateway, the way Kvasir now holds its models itself. `kvasir.json` is written with where Kvasir listens, how it knows its callers and the purposes the assistant uses, and names no model. The model you choose is added through Kvasir once it runs, and taken only once it answers from where Kvasir runs: a model Kvasir cannot reach stops an install and is said on an update. Kvasir warms and admits a local model it is given on its own, and setup waits for the admission. Until Kvasir holds the model, it is kept with its key beside `kvasir.json`, readable by nobody else, so a machine install with no services, or a Kvasir that did not come up, has it added by the next repair. The screens say Kvasir where they said the gateway.
- An update or a repair of an install from before takes the backends out of its `kvasir.json`, which Kvasir no longer starts with, and adds each back through Kvasir under the same id, with the key its key file holds. The `model.key` an earlier setup wrote is removed.
- The assistant and Kvasir are taken at the release tags this version names, Kvasir `v1.0.0-alpha.4` and the assistant `v1.0.0-alpha.23`, where they followed main, and an update fetches that tag and checks it out.
- `nils uninstall` removes Kvasir's directory with NILS, where the data is kept too: the models it holds and their keys, its subscriptions, its seal key and pepper, and the assistant's key.
- The end of setup names the model the assistant asks for, or the ChatGPT subscription.
- `nils setup` does not finish an install that would not work. Before anything is placed, the plan names what the machine lacks for it (Node 22, git and npm for the assistant; podman or docker for their runtime). While installing, a failure that leaves a chosen part unusable stops the install with the reason and exit status 1, where it was a warning under a card saying NILS was running: the desk not installed or nobody added to it, a registration at the provider not made, a place not declared, the rule packs, the assistant, its image, its gateway, its model's admission or its key, and a service that does not start. `nils uninstall` removes what was placed. An update or a repair still says these and goes on.
- A model named in `nils setup` is sent one short chat request with its address, key and name, and taken only once it answers. Otherwise the wizard says why (nothing answered, the key was refused, no such model) and asks again, or leaves the model for later.

### Fixed

- The first person `nils setup` adds to a desk is held to the desk's rules as the name and the password are typed: a username of letters, digits, dots, dashes and underscores, and a password of at least eight characters. A shorter password was taken, the desk refused it at the end, and nobody could sign in. A password typed on lines keeps its spaces, as the desk's sign-in does.

## [1.0.0-alpha.22] - 2026-09-14

Released beside nils-desk 1.0.0-alpha.22 so the two stay in step. The engine itself is unchanged since 1.0.0-alpha.21.

## [1.0.0-alpha.21] - 2026-09-14

Released beside nils-desk 1.0.0-alpha.21 so the two stay in step. The engine itself is unchanged since 1.0.0-alpha.20.

## [1.0.0-alpha.20] - 2026-09-14

Released beside nils-desk 1.0.0-alpha.20 so the two stay in step. The engine itself is unchanged since 1.0.0-alpha.19.

## [1.0.0-alpha.19] - 2026-09-13

### Added

- `POST /api/ask/documents` takes a `parent`: a document stored under another is that document's next version. A document already stored with no parent, such as a draft the assistant proposed, is adopted into the line the first time it is stored under one, and never into a line that follows it, so a person who accepts a proposal on a card keeps the card's versions in one line.
- `POST /api/ask/profile` profiles one set of a query for the desk's charts: how many subjects, sessions and stacks are under it, its stacks by their base, the values of one stack field, the sex and the age by decade of its subjects, and the kinds of their clinical events. Each part is a preview of a small query added to a copy of the document under the caller's own scope and caps, so nothing is stored. Sex and age are quasi-identifying and are withheld below the reviewer role, and a sensitive kind of event is left out below the operator role, which the answer says whether or not the set holds one. Every number counts a member once, however many of the set's paths reach it. A part that cannot be answered says why, and the others still stand.

## [1.0.0-alpha.18] - 2026-09-13

### Added

- `GET /api/sources` lists every place with the source role for the desk's Data page: how what comes in through it is handled, how many digests have read it with the first and the last, the newest of them in full (files seen, new, changed, unchanged and refused, the subjects and stacks each added, how many of those stacks are classified and how many wait for a person), and the source's totals of subjects, studies, sessions, stacks, refused files and stacks to sort. A source's subjects and stacks are the ones its digests created first, so something seen from two sources counts under the first. `?recent=` sets how many digests are given in full (12 by default).
- A place declares how what comes in through it is handled: whether it arrives identified or de-identified, and what a release does on the way out (dates kept, shifted or cut to the year, UIDs remapped or preserved, faces removed). `POST /api/places` and `PUT /api/places/{id}` take it as `handling`, refuse an unknown value with the choices, and refuse dates that move with UIDs preserved, as a release's policy does; a change is audited with what it was before. A place that declared nothing reads as arriving identified, dates kept, UIDs remapped and no defacing. The registry's schema is at version 38.

## [1.0.0-alpha.17] - 2026-09-13

### Fixed

- `nils setup` run again to change an install left a desk that keeps its own people with nobody to let in. It asked for the first person only when the desk had no store yet, and a desk that ran with nobody signing in has one. It now asks whenever the desk keeps nobody, and where nobody is added it says so at the end, with the command that adds the first. It no longer tells a person to add the first to a desk that keeps someone already.
- `nils setup` wrote the desk's configuration again only when the mode, the provider or the supervisor changed, so a desk opened to the network kept its old address, and one given the assistant never reached the gateway; in local mode the engine then refused the desk's tokens. It now sets everything setup writes whenever any of it differs, keeps the keys a person set by hand, removes the gateway's and the assistant's tables with them, and writes a file that no longer reads as TOML again whole. The plan says which of these it will do, where it said the file would be written every time.

## [1.0.0-alpha.16] - 2026-09-13

### Added

- `POST /api/supervise/folders` lists the folders inside a folder on the host, so the desk can offer a path browser. Each folder says whether the supervisor's account may open it, whether a disk is mounted there, and whether `/etc/fstab` names a disk there that nothing is mounted for, beside the disk the folder itself is on with its free and total room. With no path it says where to start: the root, the home folder, every disk mounted that holds data, and every disk `/etc/fstab` names that is not mounted. The supervisor serves one call at a time, so a folder that takes more than five seconds to read, as a network mount that does not answer may, is answered without its listing, and the answer says so.

## [1.0.0-alpha.15] - 2026-09-13

### Added

- `nils supervise` reports the install `nils setup` made and acts on it, for the desk's Home and Settings. `GET /api/supervise/install` says where the install lives, how it runs, each part's version, address and service, whether a newer release is out, and the machine's card. `POST /api/supervise/restart` restarts one part, or all in order; `POST /api/supervise/reapply` has the engine mount every source place the registry holds and starts only the engine again; `POST /api/supervise/update-all` runs `nils update --all`; and `POST /api/supervise/look` says what a folder holds before it is added. A restart, a reapply or an update runs apart from the call and records how it ended, which `GET /api/supervise/runs/{id}` reads.
- Every install `nils setup` makes runs that supervisor on its host, beside the parts, and the desk reaches it through its `[supervisor]` table, which holds a token of the supervisor's own. It runs as a systemd user unit, or a launchd agent on a Mac, whatever runtime the parts use. In docker it listens on the bridge the desk's container reaches the host by, and in podman the pod is given the host's loopback. The desk's configuration, which now holds that token, is readable by this account alone. An update and a repair start the supervisor again from the binary installed, and an uninstall removes it.
- Backups on a schedule. An admin sets it with `PUT /api/backups/schedule`: off, or every day or every week at a time of day read in the registry's timezone, keeping the newest N archives. The schedule is kept in the registry, and `nils serve --worker` with a `--backup-dir` queues a backup each time it comes round, rehearsed and kept to N; a host that was off at the hour queues it once when it is back. `GET /api/backups` lists the archives in the backup directory, newest first, each with its size, how long it took, whether it is this registry's and its last check, beside the backup place that holds the directory and when the schedule next comes round.
- `nils backup --keep N` keeps the newest N archives of the registry in the directory and removes the others, never another registry's, and `--rehearse` rehearses a restore of the archive once it is written. `nils verify --rehearse` opens every store as a restore would, without applying it: a SQLite store read only with its integrity checked, a Postgres dump read back by `pg_restore`. The outcome of a check is kept beside the manifest, and a backup or a verify run as a job records what it produced on its job row. A backup queued at `POST /api/jobs` takes both flags.
- `GET /api/settings` says the registry's timezone and the day its week starts on, with the choices, and `PUT /api/settings` changes them, for an admin. A timezone the engine does not know is refused there and by `nils settings set`, and a change is audited.

## [1.0.0-alpha.14] - 2026-09-13

### Fixed

- A backup as installed could not be made. In a container the engine saw the registry and its backups at paths of its own rather than the ones the registry's places record, so a backup started from the desk was refused as not going to a backup place, and the engine's image had no `pg_dump`, which the backup of a registry kept in Postgres needs. The containers `nils setup` writes now mount the registry and the backups at the paths they have on the machine, the image carries the PostgreSQL client, and an install on the machine itself is told when `pg_dump` is missing. `nils backup` with no `--dir` writes to the backup place the registry's place names.
- A job queued at a door, such as a digest or a backup started from the desk, waited for ever: nothing ran the queue unless someone started `nils jobs work`. `nils serve --worker` now runs the queue beside the doors, one job at a time, and every install `nils setup` writes starts the engine with it. A worker whose job runs long keeps saying it is alive, so a long digest no longer reads as a worker that stopped.
- `nils update` replaced the packs directory whole with the release's, so a pack a deployment had written for scans of its own was deleted. Only the packs the release carries are replaced now, and the update says which of the others it kept. `nils setup` puts packs in place the same way, so it keeps them too.
- The desk could not reach the gateway `nils setup` wrote: the gateway knew only its own token, refused every call the desk passed on, and the desk treated it as absent. The gateway now takes the desk's sign-in. In `off` mode nobody signs in, and in `local` mode it trusts the tokens the desk signs, as the engine does. Setup keeps its own token beside that, which needs Kvasir 1.0.0-alpha.2.
- A local model was never offered to the assistant: the gateway lists a local model only once it has passed the gateway's admission, and nothing ran it. Once the gateway answers, setup runs the admission for a local model the gateway does not list yet, where the model answers, and says which checks failed when it does not pass.
- With a commercial provider as the only model, the gateway refused every purpose, since a purpose goes to a local model unless it is mapped. Setup now maps the purposes that read no rows of the archive to the provider. Those that read rows stay closed to it until an admin opens them in the desk's settings.
- The assistant's key was made once, so a station added later was refused by the gateway. Setup makes the key again when it does not cover every purpose the stations use, or when the gateway no longer holds it, and revokes the key it replaces.
- A rerun of `nils setup` dropped every remote backend with no key from the gateway's configuration, which removed a provider whose credential is kept in the gateway and a commercial model chosen without a key. Only the example's backends that the first wizard copied in are dropped now.
- A rerun never asked about the model again, so changing it meant editing files. It now says what the assistant talks to and asks whether to keep it.
- The assistant's teaching backend was `card0`, which no gateway that setup writes has. It is now the gateway's own backend.
- A rerun of `nils setup` offered no directory of DICOM, so pressing Enter dropped the directory the install read, and naming another left the source place at its old path while the engine was given the new one. The question now offers the directory read now, and the source place moves with the answer. Every source place the registry holds, such as one added with `nils place add`, is mounted into the engine's container and given to the engine as an ingest root, as the one setup asked for is. Setup reads the places without migrating a registry that an older engine still runs on.
- With an identity provider, the engine `nils setup` wrote did not start: it was given `--auth oidc` and no issuer to trust, and the desk's `[oidc]` table was left as a comment. Setup now names the provider. It registers the desk at an Authentik with an API token, through the desk's own `register` command, or takes the issuer, client id and secret of a provider the desk is registered at already, with flags for both (`--authentik`, `--oidc-issuer` and the rest). The desk gets its `[oidc]` table and the client's secret beside it, the engine and the gateway trust the provider's tokens, and a rerun keeps the provider unless another is named.

## [1.0.0-alpha.13] - 2026-09-12

### Changed

- In a terminal, `nils setup` asks one step at a time on a screen of its own: the NILS wordmark at the top, the eight steps down the side with the answers given, the arrow keys to choose, Enter to go on, and Left or Esc to go back and change an earlier answer, which is offered again as it was left. In a narrow window the steps are a line above the question. Ctrl-C puts the terminal back and changes nothing.
- While it installs, the plan's stages are a checklist with one bar, each ticked with its time as it is done. What needs reading, such as a warning or a command to run, is shown under it.
- It ends on a card: where the desk and the engine answer, where the registry is kept, what the assistant talks to and how everything runs, each service marked as running or not, then what to run next and where the documentation is.
- Step 6 is named "What it can do".

Output that is not a terminal, and `--print`, are as before.

## [1.0.0-alpha.12] - 2026-09-12

### Fixed

- `nils update` said 1.0.0-alpha.9 was the newest release after 1.0.0-alpha.11 was out, so every install stayed at alpha.9. With every release a pre-release, GitHub has no `latest` release, and the updater took the first release the API listed, which is not in version order. It now takes the highest version listed, drafts left out. An install from before this still asks the old way once: `nils update --version 1.0.0-alpha.12`.

## [1.0.0-alpha.11] - 2026-09-12

### Added

- Step 4 can set up Postgres. "Postgres, set up here" runs Postgres 17 in a container, in podman or docker, beside the other parts. Its data is in `postgres/` in the base directory, its generated password in `postgres.env`, readable by the person alone, and it is published on this machine's loopback. The engine reaches it wherever it runs. It is offered when podman or docker answers, and `--backend postgres` without `--dsn` chooses it. "A Postgres you already run" is the connection string, as before.
- The engine's service starts after a Postgres the wizard runs in podman. `nils update --all` keeps Postgres's major version, since a new one needs its data upgraded. A repair starts Postgres again with its data and password. Uninstall removes its container, and keeps or removes its data with the base directory.

## [1.0.0-alpha.10] - 2026-09-12

### Fixed

- An install that stopped partway could not be uninstalled, because the setup record was written only at the end. The record is now written before anything is placed, updated as each part is, and marked unfinished until the install ends. `nils uninstall` removes such an install, and `nils setup` starts it again.
- With no record at all, `nils uninstall` now lists what an unfinished setup leaves in the usual places and removes it when told to: this program, the `nils-desk` and first-party packs beside it, and a base directory holding only the empty directories setup makes. Anything holding data is left.
- Declining another Postgres connection string at step 4 went on with the address that did not answer. The registry is now kept in SQLite instead.

## [1.0.0-alpha.9] - 2026-09-12

Postgres with containers, found on the first docker install that chose it.

### Fixed

- A container install made a SQLite registry when Postgres was chosen. The engine's container now makes the registry on Postgres, using this machine's address as a container reaches it: `host.containers.internal` in a pod, handed this machine's loopback, and `host.docker.internal` on docker, where Postgres must listen on the bridge. The engine's container gets the same address when it runs.
- The Postgres connection string was tried only after the plan was accepted and the images were pulled. Step 4 now tries it as soon as it is given, says whether the database answered, and offers to enter another. Without a terminal it stops there and writes nothing.
- A connection that failed said only "error connecting to server". The reason under it, such as a refused connection, is now shown.

## [1.0.0-alpha.8] - 2026-09-11

Asked for after alpha.7's first container install. It also carries a port fix found getting ready for that install.

### Changed

- Step 2 offers every runtime this machine has: on the machine, podman, and docker. Before, docker was offered only when podman was not installed, and otherwise needed `--runtime docker`. Docker is offered only when it answers. A `docker` command that is podman's wrapper counts as podman, and a docker whose daemon is not running, or that this account may not use, is left out with a sentence saying why.

### Fixed

- Only the engine's and the desk's ports were checked. A gateway or an assistant whose port something else held failed at start. Every part this machine will listen for is checked now, and no two parts are given the same port.
- "Change something" on a running install checked the install's own ports, which its services hold, and moved them. A part the setup already has keeps its port.
- The wizard's opening line said a podman install ran "on the podman".

## [1.0.0-alpha.7] - 2026-09-11

Everything with containers, and an update that takes effect. Found by removing the laptop install and getting ready to run it again with podman and docker.

### Added

- The gateway and the assistant run in containers when the engine and the desk do. They are still built on the machine, then run in Node's image with their directories mounted at the same paths.
  - **Podman:** they join the pod, where every part shares one loopback. With pasta, the pod gets this machine's loopback, so a model server on `127.0.0.1` is reached as `host.containers.internal`.
  - **Docker:** each container is reached by its name. A model server on this machine has to listen on docker's bridge, which the wizard says when you choose one.
  - **Both:** the gateway is published on this machine's loopback alone, and the assistant nowhere. The gateway starts, the key is made, and the assistant starts last. The wizard says which containers are running.

### Changed

- `nils update --all` restarts the services when anything changed, in the wizard's order, and says which are running. The same happens when `nils update` replaces the engine binary the setup record names. Until now every part kept running the old version until the next boot.
- A slow step of `nils update` is one line with a timer, with no git, npm, podman or docker output unless it fails. The same goes for the wizard's image pulls, builds and registry commands in a container.
- Source whose commit did not move is not rebuilt, and a desk that is already the newest is not fetched again.
- The assistant's service starts the assistant's own entry, which listens on loopback and exits cleanly when stopped. Flue's entry listened on every interface.

### Fixed

- A change that took every default dropped an assistant that was already installed, on a machine with no card worth serving. The question is now "Keep the assistant?", defaulting to yes.
- Docker could not build an image it could not pull. The wizard writes a `Containerfile`, which docker does not look for on its own.
- `nils update --all --check` updated the parts it was only asked about.
- A container install's engine image was left out of `nils update --all`.

## [1.0.0-alpha.6] - 2026-09-11

A way out. After the first real install was repaired, the next thing asked for was removing it, in two forms: NILS alone, with the data kept, or everything.

### Added

- `nils uninstall`, which is also the last choice in the `nils setup` menu. **NILS, keeping your data** removes the services, the programs, the first-party packs, what building the gateway and the assistant made, and the setup record. The base directory stays whole, so installing again with the same `--dir` picks it up. **Everything** removes the base directory as well, and asks for that directory's name to be typed first, because a registry's key that is gone means the same subject is never given the same code again. It shows what goes and what stays before touching anything, and `--print` stops there. Without a terminal it asks nothing and needs `--yes` to act.
- What it refuses:
  - a program the setup record does not name, itself included;
  - a pack that is not first-party;
  - a base directory that is relative, the root, the home directory or above it, or holds neither a registry nor a desk an install made;
  - bringing a docker compose project down, since another project on the same machine can share its name.
- The setup record names the programs a container install puts on the machine: the `nils` that set it up and the `nils-desk` its image was made from. A record written before this names neither, and uninstalling such an install leaves `nils` in place and says so.

### Fixed

- `nils update` did not write the version it installed into the setup record, so the wizard kept opening with the version the install began with.
- A question with no default showed an empty `[]`.

## [1.0.0-alpha.5] - 2026-09-11

The first install on a real machine with every part, in local mode. The wizard said it had finished while the gateway and the assistant were crashing in a loop. Everything that install showed is fixed here.

### Changed

- Nothing is asked after "Do it". The registry's passphrase is asked at the registry step, the first person at the sign-in step, and the model at the step that reads the graphics card, and all three are on the plan. After that there is only work to watch.
- The model is a choice a person can answer without knowing what an OpenAI compatible address is: a model server on this machine, one on another machine of theirs, a commercial provider, or decide later. Each says what to type, with the addresses SGLang, vLLM, llama.cpp and Ollama listen on. Where the address answers, the server is asked which models it serves and the name is picked from that list. A provider's key is asked for hidden and kept in a file readable by nobody else.
- The slow steps are one line each with a running timer. Git, npm and systemd output stays out of sight, and the end of a failing step's output is shown when it fails.

### Fixed

- The first person's password was read by the desk from the terminal with no prompt and echoed as it was typed. The wizard asks for it, hidden and twice, and hands it to the desk on its standard input.
- The gateway died at start. Its configuration kept the example's key file path, `/etc/kvasir/card.key`, and the example's two commercial backends that nobody chose. It now holds one backend, the one named.
- Three of the assistant's stations would have been refused: the gateway refuses a purpose it was not told of, the example declared three and the assistant uses seven. Every purpose is read from the stations themselves.
- The assistant died at start looking for a key the gateway had not made yet. On systemd the gateway starts first, the key is made once it answers, and the assistant starts last.
- The closing report named all four services as though they ran. Each unit is looked at twice, two seconds apart, and one that is not running is named with the line of its log that says why.
- The commands printed for an install without services left out the port the setup had moved to and the trust local mode needs.
- Repair now mends an install like the one that found all this: a key file that is not there and keyless remote backends are dropped, missing purposes are declared, and the assistant's key is made. A named model is never touched.

## [1.0.0-alpha.4] - 2026-09-10

Two more found the same way as the last four: by taking a path a person takes second rather than first.

### Fixed

- `nils setup --update` left the engine behind. It printed "Updating", listed the newest version in its plan, reapplied the configuration and rewrote the units, and put the engine binary back exactly as it was. On a container run the image is pulled and it was right; on a machine the engine is the binary running the wizard, and nothing above touched it, so "update everything" moved every part except the one a person is most likely to have meant. It moves the binary now, last, which on unix is a rename over an open file that the running process does not notice.
- An update that left the engine unable to classify. `nils update` refreshes the packs in use, and where there were none it said "no pack directory is in use, so none was refreshed" and stopped. True, and a dead end: an engine installed before the wizard fetched packs has none, and an update that moves the binary and leaves it unable to say what a scan is has not updated much. Where there are none it takes them, into the share directory of the prefix the binary sits in, which is where the wizard puts them and one of the places the engine looks with no flag.

## [1.0.0-alpha.3] - 2026-09-10

Installing 1.0.0-alpha.2 four ways on a machine that had never held it found four faults in an hour, and not one of them had failed a test. Each was hidden by a fallback that said nothing. This release is those four, and the fallbacks now say what they wanted and what they did instead.

### Fixed

- The image a container install pulls. A release pushes `ghcr.io/kineuro/nils:<git tag>` and a git tag begins with a `v`, but the updater strips that `v` from every version it holds, so the wizard asked for `nils:1.0.0-alpha.2`, a tag that was never pushed. Every container install fell back to building the image locally from the release binary, and said nothing about it. One function names an image now, and the run commands, the compose file, the quadlets, the state file and `nils update` all go through it. Both images are public and pull without an account.
- An engine that starts before the issuer it trusts. In `local` mode the desk mints the tokens and the engine verifies them, and the desk cannot start before the engine it proxies to: they need each other. The engine fetched the desk's keys at startup and exited when the fetch failed, which in a pod is every time and on a machine is every boot that loses the race. A JWKS file that cannot be read is still a fault in the configuration and stops the engine, because the file should be there. A JWKS URL that does not answer is a matter of order: the engine says so in a sentence naming the issuer, starts holding no key of it, and asks again when the first token arrives, with the refetch floor waived while it holds none so that token is not refused for a minute.
- The rule packs on a machine install. The binary carries none and the wizard fetched none, so an engine installed on a machine started saying `packs none` and would have refused to digest anything. The release has carried `packs.tar.gz` beside the binaries all along, and only `nils update` ever unpacked it, over a directory a fresh install had never made. The wizard now unpacks it into the share directory of the prefix the engine binary sits in, `~/.local/share/nils/packs` for the one line installer's usual place and `/usr/local/share/nils/packs` for a root install, which are places the engine looks with no flag at all, so `nils digest` run by hand finds them too. Where that prefix cannot be written they go in the registry's own directory. Failing to fetch them does not stop the install: it says what is missing, what it costs, and where to put a pack directory instead.
- A key fetch that could stall a request handler. Now that the engine survives an issuer that is not up, it asks again on a request, and that ask had no bound on it: an issuer that is unreachable rather than refusing could hold a worker for whatever the default connect timeout happens to be, and every request would pay it in turn. The fetch is bounded at two seconds to connect and four in all; the stamp that paces the asking is taken and unlocked before the fetch, so a request arriving mid-ask is refused at once instead of queueing behind the network; and while the engine holds no key of an issuer at all it asks at most once every five seconds, which is longer than a fetch can take, so two are never in flight.
- A docker install that could not write what it mounted. Podman remaps the user and `:U` hands the container ownership of the mount; docker does neither, so the image's own user could not write a directory belonging to the account that ran the wizard, and the install stopped at `Permission denied` on the registry's key store before a registry existed. Every docker run is told to be this account now: the two that make the registry, the two that run the parts, and both services of the compose file. Podman is untouched, because it already does the right thing.

## [1.0.0-alpha.2] - 2026-09-10

### Added

- `nils setup`: one wizard that installs every part, makes what they need and writes down what it did. It is the other end of the one line installer: the script fetches the binary and the binary does the rest. Eight steps, each answerable by a flag and each skippable: **what to install** (the engine alone, the engine and the desk, or everything, which adds the assistant and the model gateway); **where it runs** (on the machine as two binaries, or in podman or docker containers, detected in that order); **where it lives** (one directory holding `registry/`, `desk/`, `backups/`, `working/`, `export/`, and a directory of DICOM the engine may read, which becomes an ingest root and a `source` place); **the registry** (a key passphrase asked for twice and never echoed, through the terminal's own echo flag rather than a new dependency, and SQLite or Postgres, the connection tried before anything is written so that a wrong connection string costs a sentence rather than half a registry); **who may sign in** (nobody, the desk keeps the people, or an identity provider, one answer setting the engine's `--auth` and the desk's `mode` together, and then who may open the desk, where choosing the network while nobody signs in says plainly that anyone on that network who finds the port gets the whole registry and offers the login instead); **what this machine can do**; **keeping it running**; and **the plan**, which is a summary before a single file is written. Where a default port is taken the wizard says which and moves that part to the next free one; where the ports are free it asks nothing.
- What the graphics card can serve, said before the assistant is offered and in the product's own words. `nvidia-smi`, then `rocm-smi`, then an Apple machine's unified memory: 24 GB or more fits a 27B model at 4 bit with room for the context, which is what the stations were written against; 12 to 24 GB fits a 7B to 14B model and the stations still work with more retries on the harder questions; under 12 GB or no card at all there is no local model worth serving, and the options are a model on another machine you can reach, a commercial provider through the gateway (the prompt then leaves the machine, and the gateway marks that backend remote), or no assistant at all, which costs nothing else. The wizard never installs a model.
- Containers as a first-class choice, not a port of the machine path. A podman run is one pod named `nils` publishing only the desk's port, the engine and the desk inside it, and `:U` on every bind mount so that a rootless container owns what it reads; a docker run is a network named `nils`, the desk publishing the port, and no `:U`, because docker does not remap the user. The images are `ghcr.io/kineuro/nils` and `ghcr.io/kineuro/nils-desk`; where an image cannot be pulled a Containerfile is written beside the base directory and the image is built there from the release binary already downloaded, and the wizard says so. The registry is made by the engine either way, running the key and the init steps inside a container against the mounted volume. Podman is kept running by quadlets in `~/.config/containers/systemd/`, docker by a `compose.yaml` in the base directory, the machine by systemd user units on Linux and launchd agents on macOS; where there is no service manager the commands are printed instead. Windows is refused with a sentence and a link, not a stack trace.
- The places a fresh install declares, in the order the registry's own rule needs: the backup place before the registry that names it, then the source directory where one was given, the working directory and the export directory. An engine that serves no places door says nothing and installs the rest.
- `~/.config/nils/setup.toml` (or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME`), which is the wizard's memory: the base directory, the parts with their versions and how each one runs, the mode, the runtime, the service manager, the ports, the reachability, the backend and the places declared. Run `nils setup` again and it opens with what is installed, where, in what mode and how it runs, then offers to update everything, change something, add a part, or **repair**, which writes the configuration and the units again from what the state records, asking nothing.
- `nils update --all`: every part the state names, each in the way that part runs. A binary is replaced from its release as before, a container is a pull of the new tag and a restart of its unit with the unit rewritten to name it, a Node part is a fetch and a rebuild. One line each, and the engine last, since it replaces the binary doing the replacing. The desk's releases are its own repository unless `--channel` or `NILS_DESK_RELEASES` says otherwise.
- Prompting that survives `curl ... | sh`.
